same head do not silently reacquire that identity. Stop the client and use
a fresh device store and outbox for the restored backend, as the stopped
backup procedure requires. A different store identity still follows the
ordinary replacement path. Request captures continue to fence delayed
pages behind another owner's progress before restore detection runs.
**2026-09-15 — X1 review round 3 and the lead's confirmation fix.** `/probe`
and `/changes` report the store's committed head (never the notification
counter), and the counter only wakes polls, so a healthy store — including a
maintain rebuild commit on adoption — never reports a head below an
acquisition captured before the request. A same-identity reply from either
endpoint whose head is below the acquisition captured before that request is
therefore authoritative: it persists `E_STORE_RESTORE` through the device's
guarded observation (matching send revision and still-current capture), even
if other writes refill the head before the next sync. A delayed reply behind
another owner's newer acquisition fails the capture check and halts nothing.

**2026-09-15 — the blind reviews of lane X1 and their dispositions.** Records:
`llp/reviews/3000.007-store-replacement.*`. GPT on `f69338c00`: NOT READY — a
failed backend reload forgotten after identity persistence, unfenced
asynchronous reads, orphan receipts losing store provenance, a delayed legacy
page falsely resetting a healthy store (all High) → X1R1. Grok: READY WITH
CHANGES — equal-head replacement waiting out the long poll (High), watch
snapshots on reset, same-identity restore never converging → X1R2. GPT's
re-check of `ebb9ab3de`: 1–4 repaired; a maintain rebuild could trip a false
restore halt and a second native owner could run the old module (High) →
X1R3; Grok READY. GPT's confirmation of X1R3: both repaired; X1R3's client
relaxation let a refilled restore escape → the lead's authoritative-head
restore witness above. Ships as 0.2.18.

`/changes` reads the actual committed head, store identity and changed tables
in one read transaction. Its bounded 500-commit page invalidates all tables
when more commits remain, so reporting the head does not skip invalidations.
The in-memory sequence is only a wake signal; every assignment is monotonic,
and source adoption refreshes it after any populated maintain rebuild commit.

Each owner binds its interpreter to the adopted store identity and its loaded
native module digest, checked against durable metadata before reads,
acquisition or prediction. Same-schema, same-Quarry, same-generation replacement
cannot bless another owner's old native module. Adoption clears an obsolete
owner-local module, and module installation checks the adopted identity before
writing; the owner reloads before native execution resumes. Same-store,
same-module adoption and offline reopen retain the ordinary usable backend.

**2026-09-18 — MC0, generation discovery across an oversized sync reply.**
The bounded HTTP refusal retains the page's `generation`, `store_id` and
`watermark`. The client observes store replacement and adopts a changed
generation before shrinking or retrying that position; it never applies the
refusal as a delivered page. If a stream response still exceeds 16 MiB at
limit 1, its refusal adds `snapshot: true`, instructing a fresh paged snapshot
and catch-up with that reduced limit. An oversized snapshot at limit 1 keeps
its refusal, without restarting indefinitely. No response ceiling or commit
atomicity changes. This implements the generation-discovery prerequisite of
LLP 3000.007.001 §3.11; a request-side generation mismatch check before page
materialization remains outside this change.

### 18.3 A literal default fills the column wherever it is absent; `check` reads the store

A required column with a literal default is additive at adoption: no stored
row is rewritten, no partition event is logged, and every host materializes
the default when a stored row lacks the column, as optional columns
materialize null, before rules, indexes, validation and the wire see the
row. A `default now` or `default viewer` on a new required column is
narrowing unless the table is empty, because the value is the write's, not
the row's; the successor's message says so and its rewrite names the
literal-default and optional forms. `check` opens the project's dev store
when one exists (`.snapback4/store.sqlite`, or `--store <path>`) and prints
every narrowing step of the successor as a diagnostic at the column's or
table's site, with the rewrite, and exits 1, as `dev` would refuse the
save; a green `check` then means the running store adopts. README §5
carries the schema-evolution paragraph.

Built (lane CU4): the refusal is `E_ADOPT`, one message source for
`check` and `dev`. An unreadable store makes `check` exit 2 with the
reason rather than compile-only green.

### 18.4 The Node device takes its directory

`processDevice({ dir, binary?, spawn?, writeFile? })` from
`snapback4/native`: `dir` is the directory its stores live in; `binary`
defaults to the CLI the `snapback4` bin resolves (`SNAPBACK4_BIN`, then the
platform package); `spawn` and `writeFile` default to Node's. A call without
`dir` refuses with one sentence naming it, before anything is spawned. The
README shows the signature where it first names the opener and in the
client section's Node paragraph, and says that a module's operations sit
under `api.<module>` on the generated API. A test in the client package
requires every exported function of the public subpaths to be called in a
fenced TypeScript block of the README that typechecks against the package's
types; `processDevice` would have failed it before this landing.

Built (lane CU3): 22 exported functions had no call in the README
before this landing; each now has one that typechecks.

### 18.5 A guess costs a command, not a loop; Trending is on the card

The mechanical set (§5.5, §8) grows by the loops the 0.2.9 lane paid: the
index a read needs (`E_QUARRY_UNKNOWN` naming a `by` line, inserted into the
table's index block in whichever file declares the table); `once` written
on an argument (dropped; the modifier belongs to the column); a field
repeated in a `*` shape (dropped); the parentheses an operation with no
arguments needs (inserted). A mechanical diagnostic prints `check --fix
applies it` after its rewrite, so the command is discovered where the loop
would have been. The feeds card gains the Trending recipe of §5.3 (the
trailing day sought up to the ceiling, sorted, sliced, `complete: false`
past it) beside the maintained ranking. A helper parameter typed by a
table: where helpers are inlined per call and the argument is a row, the
parameter binds as the row; otherwise the diagnostic on a field access names
the point lookup and the shape parameter and no form is added — the lane
reports which.

Built (lane CU5): helpers are inlined per call, so a row passed to a
table-typed parameter binds as the row, and a reference keeps its meaning.

**2026-09-14 — built by lane W5.** The pinned-prefix verdict charges the reservation's static probe count once on either outcome, independent of short-circuit answers; the common attempt and verdict-before-candidate order remain. The shared helper proves all seven meters and complete observations equal for the eight denied pairs and eight true-verdict controls, on both device stores; two-probe rules charge 2 with room and reach 8193 after the 8191-probe prelude, refusing both worlds with `E_BOUND`.

### 18.6 The blind reviews of the catch-up landing, and their dispositions (2026-09-14)

GPT (`gpt-6-astra`, xhigh) and Grok (`grok-4.6`, xhigh) reviewed the
integration commit `4582f8421` blind, read-only, against `518891fd1`:
[GPT](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.gpt.md) NOT READY, twelve
findings; [Grok](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.grok.md) READY WITH
CHANGES, four. A finding one family raised was a hypothesis: each fix lane
first wrote the failing regression at `db1173ca2` (the integration plus
`origin/main`, including W5), and changed code only when it was red. Lanes
R1a (`523f30a36`) and R1b (`0807a88e7`) carried the round; the landing
branch at `056335ebf` passes the blocking suite outside the sandbox (Rust
449/0, fixtures 9/9, client 144/144).

| Finding | Family | Reproduced | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| Denied lane short-circuit: probe charge | GPT 1 | no, after the W5 merge | Already closed: the merge gave each matching lane W5's static allowance; 32 paired cases kept |
| Denied lane short-circuit: wire `tables` | GPT 1 | yes | Fixed: a rule's dependency tables come from the rule (R1a) |
| Non-uniform lane discovers hidden words, cuts later lanes | Grok H1 | yes | Fixed: a conjunct the scope pins denies the lane or page before word discovery, at the static allowance; an admitted non-uniform scope keeps its per-row cost and stays incomplete (R1a) |
| Empty device word search past a horizon reads complete | GPT 2 | yes | Fixed: horizon coverage before any early return (R1a) |
| Search state leaks into nested reads; site-keyed verdict | GPT 3 | yes | Fixed: search state passes only to the generated word lanes (R1a) |
| Audience discovery reads raw images | GPT 4 | yes | Fixed: every partition extraction reads the interpreted image (R1b) |
| Maintain grouping reads raw images | GPT 5 | yes | Fixed (R1b) |
| `sql` and `data` show raw images | GPT 6, Grok H3 | yes | Fixed: both show a required column's literal default; a device persistence export stays raw (R1b) |
| A changed default is not assessed against existing unique indexes and caps | GPT 7 | yes | Fixed: rechecked; refusal is `E_ADOPT` in `check` and activation (R1b) |
| Read-only open may change sidecars | GPT 8 | no | Not a defect: database and WAL bytes identical with a live and a stopped `dev`; a WAL reader takes part in the shared-memory read-lock protocol; the regression stays (R1b) |
| Query-wide retained marker masks a held read | GPT 9 | yes | Fixed: the marker holds only when no independent unknown or incomplete reason remains (R1a) |
| Cross-file `--fix` applies a stale diagnostic | GPT 10 | yes | Fixed: every compiled source verified, destination re-read before the write, final compile from disk (R1b) |
| Blank `dir` accepted | GPT 11 | yes | Fixed (R1b) |
| Two guidance texts generic | GPT 12 | yes | Fixed: the conjunctive per-scope rule gets the matching-merge rewrite (R1a); a reference passed to a table-typed helper names the lookup and the shape form (R1b) |
| Paired hidden worlds missing from search tests | Grok H2 | — | Fixed with GPT 1 and Grok H1: complete-observation pairs in `Cut::Cap` and `Cut::Abort` |
| README Reads bullet omits the matching merge | Grok H4 | yes | Fixed (R1a) |

The integration itself found one conflict no review saw: CU2b's client test
called the positional `processDevice` that CU3 had removed. The brief's
question 4 asked that an unreadable store degrade to compile-only; §18.3's
Built paragraph says exit 2, and both reviews read the code as following
§18.3. The loud refusal stands. The two High disclosure findings (GPT 1, Grok
H1) went back to the family that raised them for a narrow re-check before
landing.

GPT's narrow re-check of `056335ebf` confirmed findings 1–7, 9, 11 and 12
repaired and found that round 1's pre-check had opened two High defects of
its own. Grok's re-check exited after one line and was run again on the next
commit. Round 2 (lane R2, `b6499eab9`) reproduced each new finding red first:

| Finding | Reproduced | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| N1: the pre-check probed a scope-pinned conjunct row evaluation would have reached later, or never; examined rows, index entries, `capped_at`, `E_BOUND`, cursor-refusal order and `nextExpiry` differed between worlds differing only in an unreadable permit | yes | Fixed: only the leading pinned conjuncts, in evaluation order; bounds and cursor validated before any denial; expiry restored after a partial verdict; 16 paired observations identical |
| N2: a self-witness conjunct evaluated on a synthetic row hid real rows | yes | Fixed: self-witnesses simplified away before the pre-check |
| N3: a query mixing a left group's retained rows with an unknown held read loses the left rows online | yes | Documented, not built: neither side can answer that query whole; the sync card and README say to keep a left group's read in its own query, a test pins today's behaviour, and the provenance design is `issues/closed/20260914-snapback4-mixed-left-held-query-loses-retained-rows-online.md` |
| N4: `sql` projected a json column's literal default as its scalar variant | yes | Fixed: a default takes the column's projection |
| GPT 8 (again): a WAL reader may create `-shm` | — | Declined: §18.3 promises no adoption and no row writes; database and WAL bytes are identical; the shared-memory index is SQLite's WAL reader protocol |
| GPT 10 (remainder): an editor save between the final re-read and the write | — | Declined: editors take no file lock, so no fixer can close that window; every other source and the destination are checked and the final compile reads disk |

The landing branch after round 2 passes the blocking suite outside the
sandbox with no failure (Rust, fixtures 9/9, client 146/146).

Both families re-checked `c3c80fc8f`. [GPT](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.recheck-2.gpt.md)
found N1, N2 and N4 repaired and accepted the N3 and 8 dispositions, but
found that the binder nests a three-term rule as `And([And([a, b]), c])`, so
the pre-check never saw its leading conjunct (R2-1, High), and asked that
finding 10's contract say best effort. [Grok](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.recheck-2.grok.md)
found H1's admitted half still open: in a scope the viewer holds, hidden
words reaching the 32-word bound set the query's meter cut and starved later
lanes. Round 3 (lane R3, `d2fcf9cab`), the last this program allowed,
flattened conjunctions in evaluation order before choosing the leading
pinned ones; made the 32-word bound lane-local (`word_cut_at`, an operator
field kept off the HTTP reply and the card like `capped_at`), so the lane and
page read incomplete while later lanes run and real meter ceilings stay
query-wide; and made `--fix` re-read every compiled source immediately before
the write, with the README saying the check is best effort. Both families'
re-checks of `d2fcf9cab` returned READY
([GPT](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.recheck-3.gpt.md),
[Grok](./reviews/3000.007-catch-up-landing.recheck-3.grok.md)). Hidden rows
in an admitted non-uniform scope still spend the query's examined and
index-entry budget; that is the non-uniform contract, written on the search
card and in the README.

### 18.7 The result (2026-09-14)

The catch-up landed as `a5ef8da61` and was published as `snapback4@0.2.12`
and `snapback4-expo@0.2.4` (0.2.11 was another session's publish). Two
measurements followed on that pin.

The ceremony bar's fourth run
([record](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-14-snapback4-ceremonybar-0.2.12/README.md))
went 3/3 green with 4 loops (1, 2, 1), none attributed to Snapback, and
kept the natural membership rule 3/3. That matches 0.2.10. The bar groups
search by chat, so it does not exercise §18.1–§18.3.

The Astra calibration
([record](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-14-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.12/README.md);
attempt 1 aborted at M1 on a packet defect and is kept unscored) went 7 of
7 green in 5,438 s (6,890 s on 0.2.9), with 13 loops (20 on 0.2.9). It
scored 86 holistic (85). The three target categories moved from
setup_and_docs 78, data_modeling 88 and queries_and_live 74 to 79, 89 and
80. They remain below the September 5 targets (83/85, 94, 87). The
independent grade passed 101 of 119, as on 0.2.9; its nine iOS-simulator
assertions moved from fail to not-run on the same absent evidence.

What moved the notes: every finding §18 answered is gone from the scorer's
and the builder's accounts. `processDevice` without a signature, retained
history lost under a horizon, never-complete server search and the adoption
refusal all disappeared; offline search, retained departed-member history
and additive schema evolution now appear as strengths. What holds the
three categories back is two different Snapback-attributed loops:

- The presence card's prediction claim contradicts the binder's
  classification. It recurred from 0.2.10 and is charged in setup_and_docs,
  offline_sync, background_work and diagnostics.
- A helper's early `return` inside a page's shape drops the page's cursor
  while it reads `complete: true`. It is charged in queries_and_live and
  diagnostics, which fell from 88 to 78, and was reproduced in 12 rows (P1).

The builder's strict search midpoint gate missed by 0.2 ms; every other
scale probe passed. What remains is those two tickets
(`issues/20260914-snapback4-helper-early-return-drops-page-cursor.md`,
`issues/20260914-snapback4-guide-gap-presence-prediction-contradicts-residency.md`),
the `==` papercut, the mixed left/held query and the lifecycle launch
stall. N = 1, the scorer is the builder's family, and the comparison is
across runs: the 6-point queries_and_live move is the only one clearly
outside the noise.

## 19. The next landing: presence that decides effects, a helper's return, `==` (2026-09-14)

§18.7 left two Snapback-attributed loops on 0.2.12 and one papercut. The
author ruled the presence question (ruling 18, option C) and commissioned
the landing: lanes N1 (§19.1), N2 (§19.2), N3 (§19.3).

### 19.1 Presence that decides only effects does not cost prediction

A mutation is eligible to predict when every value derived from
`connected[...]` reaches only `emit` statements: an `if` whose condition
reads presence and whose taken arms contain only emits, or an emit's
payload. Effects are never predicted: on a device, `emit` fills the
runner's outbox and `local::predict` drops it. So for such a mutation the
prediction is exactly the server's row writes, whatever presence says, and
the server commits its effect decisions, including deferred grace rechecks
as specified below. A
value derived from `connected[...]` that reaches a write (its existence,
key or content), a `require` or refusal, the returned value, or control
flow that contains a write keeps the `server` site and `predicts: never`,
with a reason naming the use and the rewrite "decide only the emit with
presence". A device reads everyone as connected. Queries are unchanged:
a query that reads presence runs on
the server, and the device's answer is `complete: false`. The presence,
effects and residency cards say this in one sentence each, and LLP 3000's
decision of 2026-09-13 and §6.2's site table are reconciled to it.
Dispatch-time presence (an unconditional emit plus a service mutation that
reads `connected` when a worker claims it) stays a documented pattern for
apps that want the fresher answer.

**2026-09-15 — W5g development grace.** The registry shares the development
clock used by commits: `clock advance 36s` and `POST /dev/clock/advance`
expire an ended poll's 35-second grace, while open polls remain connected.
Production grace still measures elapsed real time. There is no additional
presence flag or stream; an online device query reads the updated server
answer and a local presence read remains unknown.


**2026-09-15 — W7b deferred grace effects.** When presence decides only
a mutation's effects, evaluate those decisions using live polls. A principal
with no live poll but an unexpired grace contributes an absence dependency:
an emitted effect depending on it is durable at commit, with `next_at` at the
latest dependent grace deadline (at most 35 seconds). Ordinary presence
queries and presence deciding rows or results still count grace as connected.
An unrelated emit is immediate. Presence dependencies follow local values and
branch control, including the lowered short-circuit setup; no device presence
knowledge or predicted row changes. The existing proof that presence reaches
only effects authorizes this different effect decision, not a second execution
of application writes.

A poll authenticated for a dependent principal during the waiting window
records reconnection durably with the queued effect. At first claim, either
that record or a currently live poll satisfies the effect: state `satisfied`,
result `{ outcome: 'presence_reconnected' }`, zero worker attempts. Even a
reconnect that ended before claim, or before restart, counts. Otherwise the
effect enters the existing worker lease protocol once; its presence condition
is then consumed, so retries remain that protocol's. Multiple grace dependencies
wait for all their deadlines and a reconnect of any satisfies the effect.
A reconnect after the window that has already ended does not satisfy it.
The authenticated poll entry and effect claims serialize under the engine lock.
Development deadlines use the development clock; production converts remaining
monotone grace to a wall-clock queue deadline. Restart retains queued deadlines
and reconnect records; the process-local development offset still resets as
documented. No live poll and no grace means an immediate emit; a live poll
still prevents an absence-conditioned emit. Mutation replay cannot emit twice.

**2026-09-14 — round 1 soundness rule.** Presence-derived values may reach
an emit through total comparisons, `exists`, boolean logic and field
construction. Arithmetic, fallible or variable-cost builtins, reads,
`new` and explicit refusals dependent on presence keep `never`, with the
use named; presence-selected variable-size values keep `never` too, since
later clones could cost more in another world. Prediction evaluates both
safe arms of each presence-dependent branch, restoring the untaken arm's
locals and discarding its effects; prediction charges the safe untaken
presence arms, and may conservatively refuse when the server would fit.
If an untaken arm needs a combined guard's value refinement, prediction
queues without a row; putting the data guard outside presence avoids that.
Effect ids use the commit sequence and emission ordinal, independently of
row ids. The binder records condition and payload ownership beside the
baseline flat statements, remapping only that side map through helper-return
lowering. Only presence analysis reads ownership; no grouping enters lowering,
admission or execution, retaining the original IR, statement sites and server
charges (round 2, 2026-09-14).


**2026-09-17 — W15a built ruling 32: held polls are unconfirmed.**
For an effects-only mutation, a held poll now supplies a five-second
confirmation deadline pinned to the mutation's commit clock. The guard
planner retains the necessary-absence proof above; a separate scalar
projection treats held counts as zero for a proven absence arm containing
an emit, including aliases, conjunctions and nested guards. Independent
conditions retain their values. The projection follows only the lowered
boolean setup needed by such a guard, while the ordinary value remains
available to payloads and other conditions. A mixed held/absent/graced
conjunction therefore takes the absence arm when its independent conditions
hold, records dependencies for the held and graced principals, and waits
for the latest deadline. An arrival within any dependency's own window
satisfies the effect. Payload-only and unproven decisions, queries,
row/result decisions and prediction retain their base values and charges.
The claim path now consults only the durable post-commit reconnect record;
a poll already open at claim cannot cancel a notification. No live poll
and no grace remains immediate; an ended poll retains its 35-second grace.
Both constants live together in the core so `Live` and other presence
providers use the same confirmation window. `clock advance 6s` expires
confirmation, and restart and replay retain the existing durable semantics.

The measured price on base `a1afab4e3625a64ffb80d19bf6cd83fc1f3a0b38`:
a 50-member send with 49 held recipients moves from **817 steps / 43,297
heap bytes / 0 effect rows / 0 dependency rows** to **1,356 / 69,400 / 49 /
49**, exactly the graced case in every meter field. Both cases charge 52
examined, 2 index entries, 3 rule probes, 27 output bytes and 1 row written.
The minimal held send moves from 6 steps / 311 heap bytes to the base
absent/graced 9 / 513; output stays 6 bytes and the other four fields zero.
Device reservations are unchanged on both stores: minimal 9 / 513 / 6;
50-member 52 examined, 2 index entries, 1 rule probe, 1,367 steps, 69,843
heap bytes, 27 output bytes and 1 row written. The separately ticketed
base two-principal reservation defect (1,967 server heap versus 1,961
predicted) is retained as an explicitly ignored regression, not changed
by this lane.

The healthy-client witness uses the commit's actual `committed.notify_all()`
and each held poll's `serve_changes` condition-variable wait; only after
that handler answers does the simulated client issue its next authenticated
poll. The 50-member fixture records exactly 49 effect and 49 dependency
rows, then 49 reconnects and 49 `satisfied` effects with zero attempts.
It also passes for a row-less commit, which a partition-only wake filter
would miss. It invokes the poll handler directly to isolate this contract from the
HTTP host's 32-waiter admission cap. A later filtered wake must retain
notification of unconfirmed dependency principals, as the existing
wake-storm ticket requires; this lane adds no per-principal wake.

**2026-09-17 — W15a review fixes and corrected meter contract (the lead's decision, §21.8 L17).**
This paragraph supersedes W7b's statements above that a live poll prevents
an absence emit and that a live poll at claim cancels it. The guarantee is
**durable admission**: a push under a proven necessary absence guard is
recorded at commit even when the recipient looks connected, deferred, and
cancelled by presence only when a new poll arrives within its dependency's
window. Delivery retains the effect's ordinary policy deadlines, worker
failures, lease retries and `--replay` suppression.

For a successful effects-only mutation, connection state never changes its
rows or result. It can change which effect arms run and therefore what the
mutation is charged, as live versus absent presence already could before
W15a; a meter refusal still rolls the mutation back. The paved
`if not exists connected[p]: emit …` form charges the same whether the
recipient is held, graced or absent. The meter contract is:

- **R1, unchanged:** prediction charges exactly the base in every field,
  on both device stores.
- **R2, unchanged:** absent, graced and unchanged forms retain their base
  arms, values and charges in every field.
- **R3′:** for a program whose presence values feed only proven necessary
  absence guards — no positive-presence arm that emits, no boolean operation
  over a presence value in a payload — held equals graced, field by field.
- **I1.** The projection reserves nothing new. Every arm a held recipient newly
  executes is a safe presence arm that device prediction already charges, and
  everything else it runs is what the base engine already runs for a connected
  recipient. A held execution can therefore exceed prediction's reservation
  **only by the base engine's own ticketed under-reservations on the statements
  it executes** — six heap bytes on a multi-principal absence guard; rule probes
  on synced reads a device trusts — and by nothing in a program that has neither.
  `held ≤ max(prediction, graced)` remains true, and asserted, for programs that
  do not compose a ticketed statement with a hedged arm.
- **R4 is withdrawn.** Universal held ≤ graced cannot hold while payloads
  keep their ordinary values and positive-presence effects keep their arms.

The review's complementary guards intentionally hedge a held recipient:
`if not online: emit notify {}` records one deferred effect with its
presence dependency; the separate `if online: emit notify {}` records one
immediate effect. Graced records just the first. A shared payload
`emit notify { online: online and true }` still reads `true` for held and
`false` for graced, and the held payload evaluates the boolean setup that
grace skips. Neither behavior is changed to force a meter equality.
With an unconditional `notes` insert and constant result `7` in each fixture,
both compositions retain identical rows and result on the server and real
device prediction entry point. Measurements on both device stores:

| Composition | Held steps / heap | Graced steps / heap | Prediction steps / heap |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Complementary guards | 25 / 2,737 | 23 / 2,630 | 25 / 2,737 |
| Shared boolean payload | 28 / 3,793 | 26 / 3,656 | 28 / 3,857 |

Each also charges 0 examined, 1 index entry, 0 rule probes, 9 output bytes
and 1 row written in all three cases. I1 holds field by field for both.
The earlier isolated necessary-absence equality tests remain in place.

**Final audit qualification, 2026-09-17:** the brief's "only known exception"
was too strong for the existing 50-member fixture too. At the exact review
base `fb63287a11e83ac0cebb14bcb443c3f07146d162`, held and graced both charge
`[52, 2, 3, 1356, 69400, 27, 1]`, while real prediction on both stores
reserves `[52, 2, 1, 1367, 69843, 27, 1]`, in the field order examined,
index entries, rule probes, steps, heap bytes, output bytes, rows written.
Thus **rule probes are 3 server versus 1 predicted**, an additional
pre-existing counterexample to the earlier server ≤ prediction wording. Devices trust synced reads
where the server performs read-rule checks (`engine/read.rs`,
`trust_synced_read` and `scan_prepared`); projection changes none of those
charges. R1/R2 prohibit repairing that difference by changing this lane's
reservations or graced execution. The existing tests continue to pin these numbers.
The round-2 rule-probe boundary reproduction is ignored with the same ticket
as the unchanged six-byte conjunction reproduction; both are one existing
server-exceeds-prediction defect class, left untouched here.
This qualification does not affect R3′: held still equals graced here.

**2026-09-17 — W15a round-2 and round-3 reviews (the lead's decisions, §21.8 L18 and L20).** I1 above
replaces the earlier universal numerical inequality, rather than claiming
that the engine reserves all server work on a device. One shared field-naming assertion checks the
minimal send, 50-member send, complementary guards, shared boolean payload
and two-principal conjunction on the simulator and both real device-store
prediction paths. The conjunction check for the scoped inequality is active; its older
server ≤ prediction reproduction stays ignored, byte for byte. The cards
and README make no I1 promise and need no edit.

The server-only setup walk uses scoped reaching-definition updates with
undo on branch exit. A join invalidates only names used before that scope
is undone; the enclosing join owns uses outside it. Read and write summaries
merge smaller sets into larger ones, rather than rescanning every descendant
assignment at every ancestor. Loop entry invalidates lazily at a name's use:
only the outermost loop entered since its definition matters, and sorted
assignment positions answer membership in that loop. Short-circuit arms
include their implicit accumulator read. The original metered proof planner, runtime
values, arm decisions and prediction charges are unchanged. Large setup
benchmarks time this unmetered pass directly: the older metered proof
planner can exhaust the unchanged heap ceiling before reaching N=4,000.
Smaller compiled mutations exercise the same families through real commits.

**2026-09-17 — W15a round-3 composition witness.**
`w15a6_composition_inherits_only_conjunction_excess` in the simulator and
`w15a6_composition_inherits_only_conjunction_excess_on_both_stores` in the
device suite measure the isolated two-principal absence conjunction and
that conjunction followed by `if exists connected[p]: emit notify {}`.
They compare all seven signed differences, held minus real prediction,
and require composition's difference to equal the isolated conjunction's;
where the isolated case has no excess, neither may composition. These
assertions stay green when the ticketed gap closes. Held may exceed both
prediction and graced here; the scoped invariant attributes that excess
to the inherited statement, without changing an arm or a charge.

Setup discovery now resolves input edges to the reaching statement's
address and marks each definition at most once, pruning independent
inputs; variable names are not definition identities across reassignment.
It performs no metered work and changes no prediction charge. At depth 24,
the independent alias DAG measured 208.905 ms before and 0.231 ms after as
a top-level guard, and 208.078 ms before and 0.233 ms after as an operand
of a necessary absence guard. Depth 200 measured 1.990 / 3.441 ms after;
no exponential depth-30 or depth-200 baseline was run.

The projection fields, scalar plumbing and setup discovery are excluded
from wasm with `cfg(not(target_arch = "wasm32"))`; wasm retains ordinary
arm selection and the proof/dependency machinery prediction needs. Gzip
falls from **470,404 to 467,632 bytes** (−2,772), still 632 bytes over the
unchanged report-only 467,000-byte budget. The simulator's cost guard pins
49 effect and 49 dependency rows; the host witness, unchanged here, owns
the actual commit wake → held-poll answer → authenticated re-poll contract.

**2026-09-17 — W15a round 4: the setup bound is O(S log S), and that is accepted.**
Round 4 (Grok READY; GPT: no High, the invariant and its witness sound, one Medium)
observed that setup discovery merges assignment summaries small-to-large, so a balanced
tree of independent arms with a distinct assignment at each of N = 2^h leaves re-inserts
N/2 names at every level: Θ(S log S) in the program's size, not the strict linearity the
lane's briefs had asked for. The lead accepts that bound (§21.8 L21): the walk is
server-only, runs once per execution over a program whose size the compiler already
bounds, and measures 1.3 ms at 1,024 leaves, 5.0 ms at 4,096, 31 ms at 16,384 and 120 ms
at 65,536 on a loaded host. `w15a7_setup_balanced_independent_tree_is_s_log_s` is a
shape-and-timeout regression for it, not a complexity proof. Stated fully (round 5): the
walk performs O(S log S) map and set operations, and its keys are generated site strings
whose total bytes P the **metered** proof planner (`plan_guards`, which runs first and
charges each site's bytes to the heap meter) has already admitted under the heap ceiling —
so comparison work is O((S + P) log S) with P bounded by an existing meter, and a program
whose site strings are too large is refused `E_BOUND` before discovery starts.
Exponential (round 1), quadratic
(round 2) and Θ(D·N) (round 3) are gone; the walk stays unmetered because a device does
not run it.

### 19.2 A helper's `return` never moves its caller's page

**2026-09-27 amendment:** [LLP 3000.027 §1](./3000.027-top-n-answer-is-complete.rfc.md#1-decision)
removes cursorless page overflow as a cause of incompleteness. A declared
`first N` or `last N`, including an auxiliary preview, is the requested
answer. Interrupted collectors and ownership of declared cursors retain the
rules below. Historical measurements in this document describe their original pins.

A helper is inlined per call (§18.5). A `return` inside it ends the helper
and yields its value; it does not end, reposition or reclassify an
enclosing page. The 0.2.12 calibration's history page lost `next` and read
`complete: true` when its shape called a helper that returned early
(`issues/20260914-snapback4-helper-early-return-drops-page-cursor.md`,
reproduced in 12 rows). The suspected mechanism is `Stmt::Return` assigning
the runner's page position from the plan of the return's own site.
Acceptance: the ticket's reproduction keeps its cursor on the server and
on both device stores; an early return in a `for`, an `if`/`else if` and a
nested helper inside a page's shape pages through every row without
duplicates; and no page whose continuation was lost reads `complete: true`.

**2026-09-14 — R2g completeness correction.** Loss is monotone across
executions within one query: a later execution of a scan site cannot clear
an earlier interrupted collector or an earlier continuation that no returned
owner carries. The returned owner keeps the existing last-execution rule for
`next`; that cursor never acquires ownership of an earlier execution. An
exhausted execution needs no cursor owner. The same rule applies to plain,
matching and merged pages on the server and both device stores.

An exiting loop counts as a collector only when its writes flow to a returned
value, through aliases, records or enclosing loops. Traversing an intact
returned page for an auxiliary calculation does not interrupt that page,
even if the traversal breaks; a separate returned collector built from that
same page can still lose rows. The ordinary meter, rules and horizon continue
to determine the other reasons a query is incomplete.

**2026-09-14 — the third review round, settled by the lead.** A `return`
inside a loop exits every enclosing loop with the result, so each enclosing
loop is a collector even when no write precedes the return
(`for i in items[] after c last 5 by byN: return [i]` over four rows now reads
`complete: false`; it read `complete: true` after R2g). The flow analysis only
removes a registration for a loop it visited and proved auxiliary; a loop it
never reached (inside a merge lane's setup or an expression block) stays
registered. A page that never executes because an earlier return or break
left its enclosing loop lost nothing: its rows were never read. One
conservative case remains and is accepted: when the same scan site runs
again after its earlier interrupted output was discarded (an accumulator
reset between iterations), the earlier loss still holds, so a result that
re-collected the whole page reads `complete: false`. The error is always
toward `complete: false`, never toward a lost page reading complete;
distinguishing a discarded execution would need per-value provenance, which
this landing does not build.

### 19.3 `==` is a guessed spelling with a mechanical rewrite

`a == b` is refused with `E_QUARRY_SYNTAX: equality is = in Quarry`, the
rewrite `=` (the operator, as `//` → `--`) that `check --fix` applies, and no cascade of further
errors from the same line. No alias enters the parser (ruling 3).

### 19.4 The blind reviews of the next landing and their dispositions

Both families reviewed the N1–N3 integration `fde962314` blind; each
re-check read only its own family's earlier output. The records are
`llp/reviews/3000.007-next-landing.*`.

| Round | Commit | GPT | Grok | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `fde962314` | NOT READY: effects displaced row ids (1); presence-dependent refusals and meter cuts (2); the block wrapper bypassed population admission (3); short interrupted pages and lost siblings read complete (4); exact-bound scalar reads incomplete (5); the wrapper changed meters and sites (6) | READY WITH CHANGES: the same admission bypass (High); a short interrupted page read complete (Low); a two-block `where:1` charge (Low) | R1p (commit/ordinal effect ids, the totality rule, the untaken-arm charge, statement setup before admission; the lead mapped a meter cut after an untaken charge to a queued prediction) and R1g (per-page loss) |
| 2 | `33b530d41` | NOT READY: 1, 2, 3, 5 repaired; A (High) a repeated scan site erased an earlier loss; B (Medium) an auxiliary break poisoned an intact returned page; C (Medium) helper-return lowering still changed IR | READY | R2g (monotone loss, returned collectors only, a generated-shape oracle) and R2p (ownership in a binder side map; executed IR byte-identical to `ce15819ad` over 3,219 programs) |
| 3 | `4c1e825bc` | NOT READY: A, B, C repaired; D (High) a direct `return` from a page loop read complete; E (Medium) a discarded re-execution keeps its loss | three attempts exited without a review | The lead (`ba40649ef`): a return registers every enclosing loop and unvisited loops stay registered, red/green on the server and both stores; E accepted as conservative in §19.2. The three-round limit closes review here |

While the round-3 suite ran, `chat's real TypeScript invite worker is
supervised by dev` went red: an orphaned claim long-poll of a killed
worker could lease the next request to nobody. `dev` now gives each worker
child its own session, closed on exit (`0380b3ae6`, journal). The landing
merged `origin/main` (0.2.13, published by the performance program, and
the excellence program's prepared 0.2.14) and ships as 0.2.15.

## 20. Agent ergonomics: everything the Astra runs charged (2026-09-14)

The author set the goal on 2026-09-14: "solve every agent ergonomics problem
with snapback4 holding back the astra medium clique scores." An evidence
inventory of the 0.2.6, 0.2.9, 0.2.10 and 0.2.12 Astra lanes, the ceremony
bars and the September 5 competitor cells found the scorer grading defects
and misleading documentation, not features. Snapback 4 scored 87 on
setup_and_docs and 91 on diagnostics on 0.2.6; it scored 79 and 78 on 0.2.12,
charged for two defects the builder blamed on Snapback (§19.1, §19.2) and one
missing card. Each decision below anchors a lane of the next landings. The
inventory's identifiers (S01–S36) are cited per decision. Items on
`NOT-DOING.md` (ephemeral typing, relevance ranking) and items that would move
a ruling are not decisions here; they are questions for the author.

### 20.1 A word finds its card, and prediction has one (S01, S19, S20, S21, S04)

`snapback4 guide <word>` prints a card on stdout for every word a builder
reaches for. Aliases map to their card: `prediction`, `predict`, `offline`
and `optimistic` go to a new `prediction` card; `pagination` and `cursor` go
to `reads`; `migration` goes to schema evolution; `sql` goes to the inspection
verb; `push` and `notification` go to `effects`. A word with no card prints
the nearest cards on stdout and exits non-zero. The `prediction` card says
what makes a mutation predictable, with compiled examples:
- held, horizon and server sites, with `why <op>` and `check --residency`;
- a client-minted id already dedupes, so an `exists t[id]` guard past the
  horizon only costs prediction;
- `upsert` predicts an update and never an insert for a row the device lacks;
- presence decides only effects (§19.1).

Cards whose set-state recipes use `upsert` say how they predict. The writes
card carries a recipe for server-arrival order (a per-group ordinal kept by
the mutation). The README's sign-in section carries an onboarding recipe:
check handle availability by query, and treat a session with no profile as
onboarding.

### 20.2 The CLI and client stop inviting mistakes (S02, S03, S22, S28, S32)

- **Assets:** the README's React asset example narrows `status` and renders
  the image, and typechecks.
- **Sign-out:** the `Client` type a builder holds exposes sign-out, or the
  README names the type that does.
- **init:** `init --online`/`--expo` merges into an existing `package.json`
  instead of refusing it.
- **Replayed writes:** a replayed write's result is typed as absent,
  following `issues/20260914-snapback4-write-result-type-omits-replay-state.md`.
- **Replay flags:** `dev --replay` suppresses new effects and holds schedule
  admission without app code (W5a, 2026-09-15; calibration G2). Emission keeps
  its evaluation and meter; a successful mutation or job chunk increments a
  durable suppression count in the same transaction instead of storing an
  effect payload. Refusals and duplicate mutations add no count. `doctor`
  reports `suppressed`; restarting with or without replay never delivers
  these effects. The dev store retains the policy for local `run` until the
  next host start, which sets it from its flags; a normal start clears the
  policy and keeps the count. Replay skips held schedule periods on release
  and already queued jobs still run. Existing effects keep their ordinary
  lifecycle. `--hold-effects` only pauses claims and keeps effects claimable
  later; `--hold-schedules` only holds admission. `--replay` is one spelling
  for the no-notifications replay task, rather than a second independent
  suppression flag. It is refused outside `dev` or with `--release`
  (`E_REPLAY_DEV_ONLY`); production always emits normally.
- **Expo:** `init --expo` writes the Metro configuration the web device's wasm
  needs.

### 20.3 A Following feed that is complete and cheap is on the card (S14, S15)

Every Astra run rebuilt Following differently, and the 0.2.12 grader found
its read-time filter returning short incomplete pages at scale. The feeds
card carries one paved Following feed. It is complete at the scale envelope
(1,000 users, a 500-follow reader, sparse readers) and answers on the device.
Following a user acquires that author's recent posts; unfollowing removes
them; reposts and the reader's own posts appear. `check` notes a `where`
filter whose predicate pins nothing in the scanned index prefix, because at
scale such a filter returns short incomplete pages, and the rewrite names the
recipe. The lane records the design it chose, and the alternatives it
measured, in this section.


**Built by lane C, 2026-09-14: choose the hybrid.** The simulator seeds
1,000 author identities, exactly 50,000 source events and 1,415 follow
edges: independent 0/1/5/500-follow readers, a celebrity with 900 followers
and 200 posts, a celebrity-plus-four reader, and a five-follower writer.
All three candidates run in the same interpreter with the default meters.
Each latency is the nearest-rank p95 of 20 samples after three warm-ups,
in milliseconds on the shared development host; these are simulator
observations, not HTTP or competitor claims. Cold rows come from actual
partition snapshots. Index entries are the existing `Metered.index_entries`
counter (plain row scans charge `examined`, hence zero in the read column).
The comparison is an explicitly run ignored measurement test; the selected
recipe's envelope and both-store device tests run in the blocking suite.
Bulk fixture loading removes caps only during seed acquisition, then adopts
the original schema to validate every row, unique index and cap before any
query, write or snapshot is measured.

The old unscoped filter returns 4/20 and 8/20 for the zero- and one-follow
readers, both `complete: false` at 8,193 examined (the attempted charge
that crosses the 8,192 ceiling). The five-follow case fills 20 only after
7,969 examined. The merge fills every page but visits up to 501 lanes and
costs 12.60 times the five-follow read in this run. Full fan-out reads one
prefix but its celebrity acknowledgement costs 79.63 times the
five-follower acknowledgement. The hybrid has one private lane plus one
lane per pulled author: 22 examined without a celebrity, 45 with one,
and two writes for a celebrity post. The first hybrid latency sample set
contains host-load outliers (the identical 22-row one-follow read took
3.775 ms); its celebrity read exceeded the 3× ratio and is repeated alone
below rather than counted as passing on that sample.

The isolated hybrid repeat (`SB4_FEED_CANDIDATE=hybrid cargo test -p
snapback4-sim --test clique following_candidates_at_scale -- --ignored
--nocapture`) measured read p95s of **0.364 / 0.343 / 0.356 / 0.373 /
0.577 ms** for 0 / 1 / 5 / 500 / celebrity-plus-four follows. Ordinary and
celebrity acknowledgement p95s were **1.664 / 0.637 ms**. The dense/5-follow,
celebrity/5-follow and celebrity/ordinary-write ratios were **1.05× / 1.62× /
0.38×**, all below 3×. Every read again filled 20, was complete, and had
identical deterministic meter counts to the table. The noisy first sample
is retained above; the deterministic counts and both-store correctness
assertions, not a wall-clock threshold on a shared host, are the regressions.

The recipe promotes an account permanently on its 51st follower, adding
pull rows for its current followers and itself in that same transaction.
Each event's delivery mode is immutable: earlier events remain pushed and
future events broadcast, so promotion creates no duplicate. Follow copies
the newest `authors.max(200).min(917)` source events only when they were pushed
(§20.19: sized with the caps in wave 10, then bounded by the 1,024-row mutation
write ceiling less a 107-row promotion allowance); shared events are
read directly. Unfollow deletes all private rows by that actor and its pull
row atomically; the viewer's own original and repost remain. Backfill of
200 rows measured 207 examined / 1,405 index entries / 201 written; the
matching purge measured 204 / 1,003 / 201. Repeating follow, unfollow or
repost inserts nothing twice. The 220-post lifecycle case checks the exact
backfill boundary and pagination across promotion, unfollow and refollow.

Two small additions make this possible without changing audiences or
horizon semantics. A merge lane may name a second table with the header's
columns and the same plain index; each scan still uses that table's own
rule and residency. The first lane owns the existing cursor descriptor;
the interpreter validates it before discovery and translates it for the
other table. Serialized values, IR variants and cursor encodings do not
change. IDs identify an item across projections; copies keep their
ordering fields consistent. `cap N` with no `by` uses the existing cap's
empty prefix to bound an entire table. Both forms are on the language
card, and positive/refusal regressions were red against the pre-change
implementation at `33b530d41b00f1d685656f360e37c52ec1255ad6` before passing.

**2026-09-14 — review round 1, lane W2R1m:** the shared cursor is withheld
when a merge encounters a non-uniform read, a meter cut or a word-limit
cut. An unfinished lane may still contain a readable row ahead of the
last returned key; advancing every lane to that key would skip it. The
non-uniform class withholds `next` even within budget, so hidden rows
cannot change cursor presence by exhausting the meter. Uniform merges
within budget keep their existing pagination, including equal ordering
keys and projection deduplication. This applies to ordinary, cross-table
and matching merges on every host; no serialized cursor form changes.
Merged cursors bind the table, index, direction and typed ordering suffix, not
the generation: a matching descriptor continues through the current data
after adoption. The feeds card states that contract.
**2026-09-17 — wave 13, lane W13a (LLP 3000.007.000):** the meter-cut arm gains
one exception — a cut while *draining* a uniform lazy merge, every head known,
records `next` after its last emitted row; an opening cut, a word cut and the
non-uniform class stay withheld, and the class is now applied when the outcome
is built, so the rule holds in both statement orders. The merged position
carries the row id when the suffix lacks it; the serialized v1 form is
unchanged, and a token minted for a position without the id is refused.
§20.22.5.
Direct table and maintain page cursors bind the evaluated prefix and active
generation under §20.9; their tokens are refused after adoption.

Private inboxes and public broadcasts sync in full under declared caps:
50,000 private rows per reader, 32,000 broadcasts total, 1,000 source events
per actor, 1,000 follows per account and 32 pulled authors per reader.
This avoids the exhausted-horizon ambiguity even for an empty lane. Caps
refuse an over-limit write atomically; archiving or a larger application
design is required beyond them. The public broadcast projection reaches
every device (200 rows in this envelope); raw posts and the follow graph
stay online. This makes no subset-public-sync or union-audience claim.
The rejected private-group prototype stopped at the existing grant guard;
that authorization rule is unchanged. There is no job or exact horizon
maintenance. This replaces the chronological Following recipe described
by LLP 3000.004; that document's ranked-feed recipe remains unchanged.

`N_SPARSE_FILTER` is limited to row-dependent, unscoped paged query scans
without a seek or matching prefix, whose predicate pins no leading index
column. In an `or`, both branches must pin that column to avoid the note;
a scoped prefix or seek avoids it. Its rewrite names the feeds card.
The card and README compile without a diagnostic or note. Both device
stores match server data and cursor pages at the envelope, and stream the
follow-time backfill and unfollow purge. Dense acquisition of 25,250 rows
measured 3,130.977 ms on SQLite and 2,826.078 ms on memory, after a
765.905 ms server snapshot; those wall times include the device apply calls.

**2026-09-15 — W7a: installed authority and synchronous prediction.**
The 0.2.22 calibration exposed a ghost source reference through the prefixed
recipe's standalone `feedCreatePost`. A prefixed install now converts every
standalone mutation into a `fn`; the app owns all client mutations and validates
or creates its post before inserting a reference event. Only Following remains
a client query, and the purge remains a system job. Unprefixed standalone
reference-event programs retain their operations.

Restoring synchronous offline profile save, post creation and follow backfill
requires more than syncing author modes: the follow graph and source references
are read by those helpers too. The recipe syncs these already-public rows and
makes pull routing public, derived from the public graph. Global caps bound
this metadata to 1,000 authors, 100,000 edges, 60,000 source references and
32,000 pull routes; the existing per-account and engine meter ceilings remain.
Private inboxes retain their complete capped partitions. Copy inserts rely on
a fresh event or a new follow rather than an `if absent` probe into another
reader's private inbox. Predicting loops cover the declared group cap; the
backfill stops after the newest `authors.max(200).min(917)` events — a fixed 200
until wave 10 sized it with the caps, and bounded since by the mutation write
ceiling so a follow cannot refuse `E_BOUND` inside the declared envelope (§20.19).

This pays acquisition for synchronous prediction: the wired 500-follow witness
acquires **3,095 rows / 335,403 JSON payload bytes**, previously 1,055 rows.
The 50,000-event recipe envelope acquires **78,566 rows**, including 25,050
private entries, for 500 follows. The card publishes the measured table counts;
this change makes no cold-start reduction claim. Following remains 20 complete
entries with 82 examinations and 21 probes in the wired 5/500 witness.

**2026-09-16 — W11a, promotion preserves every existing delivery (author's lane brief).**
This changes W7a's intentional policy of skipping saturated followers. Promotion
requires successful route installation for every follower, including the triggering
reader; a follower at 32 pulls refuses the entire follow/promotion transaction with
`FEED_PROMOTION_PULL_CAP`. App and recipe edges, author mode, routes and counts roll
back together, leaving existing pushed delivery intact. Show the refusal; each
saturated follower must unfollow a shared author to free a slot before retrying the
refused follow with a new write ID. If they cannot, decline the new follow and keep
pushed delivery. More than 32 shared authors needs a separate serving design, not a
cap increase. A new follow of an already-shared author without a slot still refuses
`FEED_PULL_CAP`. Requiring route success adds no writes: the 107-write promotion
allowance and 917-event maximum backfill stay unchanged.
A saturated author's own shared events get an own-inbox copy. Edges record
`at: now`, with `0` as the default for imported edges of unknown age.

### 20.4 A queued web write survives navigation (S29)

On the web, admission waits for the outbox's IndexedDB commit. `pagehide`
or becoming hidden sends the oldest durable unconfirmed write with an
authenticated keepalive request, including a retry of that same head when
an ordinary send is in flight. A blocked or oversized head is never skipped;
requests across clients share the browser's **64 KiB body budget**. Unsent
or unconfirmed writes stay durable for retry on reopen with the same mutation
ID, so replay cannot apply a write twice. If the exit response has not finished
after **one second**, ordinary flushing retries the same durable head before
advancing. `close()` stops waiting immediately without cancelling keepalive.

Admission that finishes while the page is suspended rechecks exit transport
after its commit. Exit notifications wait for the local session-lock queue
to drain, including a turn queued after admission; notification itself holds
no session lock, so peer logout and transport's authority checks can proceed.
On reopen, a matching server store identity and
non-regressed head allow unknown writes to replay before the first sync
finishes; §18.2's replacement and restore rules still apply.

**2026-09-15 — W4a, synchronous pre-admission journal.** Before `mutate`
returns its promise, a web host holding its exclusive persistence claim captures the
mutation id, operation, arguments, minted row ids and submission order in
localStorage. That synchronous store survives same-origin navigation; no network request exposes an unadmitted write.
On open, its records enter the existing IDB outbox in order before new writes.
A failed admission save leaves the record recoverable before a surviving page's
next admission too. A closed tab's records use the same exclusive claim and orphan
adoption rule: the next device opening the store adopts them, never a live tab's.
Recovery and ordinary admission allocate queued submission numbers from the same
device counter in the serialized host chain, including recovery deferred until
backend adoption. Capture order also advances past the journal already held.
Already-queued primary writes precede recovered orphan records.

Under the orphan's lock, a matching healthy destination installs its authenticated
backend before recovering records left behind `backend_required`. An orphan with
no kept backend or store identity is skipped, leaving its journal intact for a
later matching open; no identity is guessed from its journal key. Malformed or
unusable orphans are isolated so healthy stores still open, and one malformed
journal key cannot hide other tabs. A failed destination commit still discards
its uncommitted VM. Skipping neither loses a record nor makes it executable: the
usual identity fence and transfer acknowledgement must succeed on a later open.

An IDB transfer marker shares the admission transaction and suppresses duplicate
admission by mutation id against queued writes and receipts, including after a
receipt leaves the outbox while localStorage removal is failing. It contains no
server result or terminal settlement. Successful transfer removes the synchronous
record; acknowledged markers are collected when that removal succeeds. A
live admission's prediction or validation refusal keeps the ordinary failed outcome
even if bookkeeping fails. It removes the journal record, replaces it with a
non-executable cancellation, or acknowledges it in IDB. If neither store can
persist cancellation, recovery is indeterminate: an executable record may remain
and later be admitted if the authored condition changes. A returned refusal does
not promise preserved cancellation when both stores fail. Original arguments
and minted ids survive recovery. Sends remain exclusively the outbox's, in its existing order, with the
original mutation id; there is no new exit ordering or settlement path.

Recovered, previously unadmitted records keep their source backend store identity
until they leave the outbox. An identity mismatch retires them without sending
them to the replacement, including after an offline reopen and orphan adoption.
This is stricter than §18.2's treatment of ordinary already-admitted unknown
writes, which is unchanged. Same-store restore refusal still halts sending.

The journal is bounded to **32 records and 256 KiB** of UTF-8 keys and values
per device store, and **64 KiB per serialized record**. Unavailable, throwing or
full localStorage, an unidentified backend, exceeded bounds, or
browser storage clearing leave that write's unawaited navigation **best-effort**:
the write can still be lost before IDB commits. A journal-only write needs another client to
open before it can be admitted and sent. Keepalive remains best-effort and sends
only admitted writes. These are navigation/reopen guarantees, not a claim about
physical power loss. Built-in IndexedDB now refuses opening without Web Locks.
Custom providers must supply an exclusive claim; alternate names must use the
requested store's `:tab-` namespace and support discovery/removal so closed
stores can be adopted. Non-alternate claims must name the exact requested store. For programmatic
navigation, `await client.mutate(...)` remains the strongest durable boundary; handle `failed` before leaving.
`pending` means IDB admission is durable and the server outcome is unconfirmed.
The writes card and README state these limits.

**2026-09-16 recovery correction.** A surviving executable journal record is
an indeterminate intent. Recovery uses the existing replay-candidate admission
path: successful local prediction is retained, but a local refusal becomes an
unpredicted queued entry whose outcome the server decides. Stale rows in either
the primary or an orphan cannot permanently cancel it before catching up.
Live admission still returns its ordinary refusal and attempts cancellation;
already transferred/cancelled records are skipped before recovery. Store binding,
original IDs, durable FIFO and replacement fences remain unchanged. This needs
no new device wire operation or cross-platform host callback.

The Node model covers same-turn teardown and an unresolved admission save, one
and two writes, original row ids, order and at-most-once application, stale
journals with queued and retired receipts, refusal, storage failure and bounds,
closed/live tabs, and store replacement. W4R1a adds deferred backend recovery
followed by an offline successor (ABC), backend-required and unusable orphans,
independent cancellation-store failures, absent Web Locks, malformed-key
discovery, queued-primary-before-orphan order, and an unidentified backend.
The lead's real Chromium run of
`witness/navigation-immediate.mjs` (chromium-1243, outside the sandbox) passes
**10 of 10**, against the **8 of 10** residual at 0.2.19 recorded in §20.12;
both unawaited zero-delay cases apply exactly once.

**2026-09-16 — W11R1a, terminal receipts have shared custody.** Before an
adopted tab store can be removed, its value-free terminal write history is
committed to `${name}:receipts`, where `name` already binds server and viewer.
A separate, nonwaiting Web Lock serializes merges into one record bounded by
`WRITE_HISTORY_LIMIT` (256); existing shared ids keep their outcome and position,
and new recovered ids append in recovery order. Every opener and receipt lookup
reads that window alongside its own device window without copying or refreshing
it. The shared record contains no pending state, outbox, arguments or results.
A failed shared commit leaves the source intact. Winning adoption therefore
cannot move a terminal receipt out of reach of a replacement tab; ordinary
bounded eviction and browser storage loss remain possible.

A held store lock remains the ownership authority; there is no reliable separate
live/dead signal. Adoption takes one nonwaiting attempt per candidate per pass,
with no foreground retry sleep. **2026-09-16 — W11R2:** busy candidates now use
one cancellable queued Web Lock request per lock as a release signal, replacing
the one-second background poll. Recovery rechecks current stores after the lock
releases; a live or suspended owner's lock is never stolen. Unidentified or
structurally unresolved journals wait for an open or identity/sync signal, and
persistence failures get at most three retries after 1, 2 and 4 seconds per open.
Receipt lookups first test whether the shared window exists and never create it.
Closing cancels waits and retry, and an adopted outbox wakes ordinary client
flushing even when no backend change arrives. Deterministic tests force A to remove B before C
opens, hold a candidate past the completed foreground open, and exercise sent
and failed receipts, exclusive queued writes, failed shared persistence,
concurrent adopters, immutable duplicates and the 256-entry bound.

**2026-09-28 — Worker execution and page authority (SS rounds 4–5).**
The journal now separates its page-local capture, authority and cleanup from
VM execution. The same admission, exit-recovery and binding plans have
synchronous and asynchronous executors; original ids, captured order, arguments,
minted ids and authority remain in the page's record. The existing default
opener retains synchronous admission and exit dispatch.

The internal Worker host runs the unchanged wasm and strict IndexedDB
adapter together. Each private call must commit before its result can return;
the `j` transfer marker and outbox change share that transaction. Rows, staged
images and full persistence deltas stay in the Worker. Round 4 introduced that
foundation; round 5 integrates the public opt-in described below.

The page retains the exclusive store claim. Before any persistence, the Worker
acknowledges a second lifetime Web Lock named for that claimed store. A
replacement Worker page uses the same lifetime name, so navigation cannot race an old
Worker that has not stopped yet. On failure or explicit termination the page
waits for that lifetime lock, then an IndexedDB read transaction behind prior
writes, before restoring from durable state. Close after termination also
restores under the claim before releasing ownership. A failed barrier remains
sticky; a later close cannot forget it and release the claim.
`terminate()` itself is not a persistence acknowledgement. A lost reply after
commit rejects the call; journal replay discovers its durable marker instead
of admitting the same id again.

Chromium qualification and remaining work are in
[SS round 4](../snapback4/reports/20260927-ss-sync-scale/ROUND4.md).
SS round 5 adds a control snapshot after strict durability: store/generation,
acquisition incarnation, prediction tables (unknown blocks), queue revision,
issued-time floor and restore/send fences. Synchronous journal capture fences
page reads before transfer; only that id's committed acknowledgement releases
it. An older Worker reply cannot clear a newer capture. The local query path
can use this internal capability for already server-answered cold watches;
held reads, composition and supplementary delivery collection stay disabled
on that bypass. Store/head, credentials, expiry and final-publication fences
still apply. The ordinary opener supplies no such capability and is unchanged.

The internal suspension helper durably prepares the bound oldest head's
`begin_send` before giving the page dispatch permission. Every subsequent
transaction invalidates that permission synchronously at enqueue, including
`sync_state`; old network captures retain the preceding send revision and
are refused. Dispatch checks this permission and the page control snapshot,
then captures the exit journal synchronously before keepalive. If preparation
is stale or capture fails, the head stays durable for ordinary recovery.
Preparation runs on outbox changes, not after every sync capture. The public
adapter integrates this helper; a busy pagehide with stale preparation retains
its durable head for recovery instead of dispatching it.

The internal adapter also recovers orphan journals under the orphan's page
claim and Worker lifetime lock, transfers queued custody once, and commits
shared terminal receipts before deleting the source. Full row images stay
inside the source Worker. Delivery's conditional CAS transfers only its
`device:delivery:` metadata delta to the page; the existing strict IDB
transaction checks the page's `current()` callback immediately before commit.
Abort discards the Worker VM and restores from durability before reuse. No
callback is cloned and no earlier page check substitutes for that commit.

The opt-in is now `webDevice({ worker: true })`, with the ordinary host still
the default. Both openers wait for a preceding Worker's lifetime and IDB
barrier after taking the page claim, including mixed navigation. The packaged
entry supports the default offline wasm cache and supplied URLs, bytes,
Response promises and compiled modules. Injected persistence providers stay
on the ordinary host; `worker:true` with `persist` explicitly refuses rather
than cloning callbacks or silently moving that provider to another realm.
An empty offline open checks only backend metadata under the exclusive,
quiescent claim, avoiding a failed VM startup before first installation. A
temporary control barrier keeps cold-watch eligibility but sets prediction
authority to unknown: it withholds delivery without queuing a local read.
Round 5's repeated Chromium measurements establish responsive 1,000-conversation
watches, but acquisition still exceeds the 27 s target and the 100-conversation
control. No guide recommendation or landing qualification follows from these
tests. Measurements, failures and outstanding review are in
[SS round 5](../snapback4/reports/20260927-ss-sync-scale/ROUND5.md).

**2026-09-28 — SS round 6, real-app acquisition.** The Clique web export
and copied full-scale store are now the measurement workload; the largest
account is selected from actual memberships. IndexedDB publication keeps
the existing bucket format and strict transaction. A pure deletion delta
may replace per-row overflow deletes with a range delete only after a read
in that transaction proves every existing physical key belongs to the
deletion set. The read stops at the number of unique requested deletions
plus one; an unrelated survivor, a mixed put/delete delta or an incomplete
proof keeps individual deletes. Packed bucket contents are still updated
individually. Pure bucket deletions need no insertion-capacity calculation,
so removed images are not re-encoded to measure their old size. Row images,
watermark, outbox and staging changes retain one abort/commit boundary.
The wasm ABI, index implementation and budget are unchanged; a measured
sorted bulk-index candidate exceeded the budget and was discarded.
The sync card explains per-group multiplication and public inherited
interaction views; `N_MAINTAIN_SYNCED` already names Clique's two views.
Measurements, rejected candidates and remaining review requirements are in
[SS round 6](../snapback4/reports/20260927-ss-sync-scale/ROUND6.md).

### 20.5 `upsert` predicts its insert when absence is provable (ruling 19; S20)

On a device, `upsert t[key] { … }` whose target the device does not hold
predicts the insert arm when two conditions both hold:
- **(a) The group is held with no horizon.** `t` is synced, and the key's
  group has no horizon, so the device holds every row of that group this
  viewer may read.
- **(b) The key decides readability.** The key determines readability, so no
  row hidden from this viewer can exist at that key. Examples: a read rule
  `public`, or `.col = viewer` with `col` in the key.

Otherwise the device queues without a row, as §8.7 (e) requires, and never
predicts `NOT_FOUND` or an insert for an unheld target. `check --residency`
and `why <op>` say which applies; the prediction card states the rule. Likes
and reactions keyed by `(post, viewer)` predict their first write offline.

### 20.6 A read rule may name a service principal (ruling 20; S35)

A read rule may carry disjuncts `viewer = 'service:<name>'` beside its
audience rule. Services hold no partition, and the `service:` namespace is
reserved, so the sync audience is inferred from the rule with those
disjuncts removed; no device's partition changes. `check --residency` lists
the service grants. A second disjunct that is not a service principal is
still refused.

### 20.7 A public table may sync a subset to each device (ruling 21; S16)

A table whose read rule is `public` may declare a `sync` audience narrower
than the read rule (`sync to .followerId`, or an audience through another
table). A device then holds a subset of what its viewer may read. A read the
subset cannot decide is server-class, like a read past a horizon:
`complete: false` on the device, answered by the server online. A write to a
row inside the subset predicts as usual. `NOT-DOING.md` records the move in
the same change. The public-audience half of the exact-audience invariant
("never misses one it may") is given up deliberately for these tables; the
other half ("never holds a row it may not read") stands. The work lands
after wave 2.

### 20.8 The darwin binary is signed with Developer ID (ruling 22; S31)

`scripts/publish.mjs` signs the production darwin binary with the
Developer ID Application identity, with hardened runtime and a secure
timestamp, verifies the signature, and refuses to publish if signing fails.
It notarizes when a notarytool keychain profile is configured; otherwise it
says in its output that the binary is not notarized, never silently. Launch
latency under load is measured for ad-hoc against Developer ID signing and
recorded in the journal, because whether signing removes the `_dyld_start`
stall is not established.

### 20.9 Maintains that filter, compute group keys and expose an order (S24, S25, S26)

**Design proposal, 2026-09-14, lane D-design (Codex / gpt-6-astra).** The
author commissioned this section; its recommendations are not additional
rulings under §14. The implementation lane follows the current landing.
The language card and package README still describe the shipped language.
This section changes neither of them and implements no production code.

#### 20.9.1 The work the application had to own

The read-only inventory is
`/Users/ccheever/projects/snapback-next-20260914/INVENTORY.md`, S24–S26.
Its L12 evidence is the Astra lane at
`/Users/ccheever/clique-sb4-20260914-astra-0.2.11/lanes/astra-snapback4/`
(the directory name predates its final 0.2.12 pin). In that lane:

- `snapback/insights.q` counts original posts by summing an `original`
  0/1 column, groups by stored `day` and `digestBucket` columns, counts
  received likes by the `authorId` copied onto each like, and stores
  repost/reply attribution separately. `summary()` builds all seven UTC
  days explicitly, including days whose maintained scalar is zero.
- The same file declares a handwritten `ranked` table, indexed by score
  and post time, expiring after the post's trailing day. `social.q` updates
  it when posts and likes change. It includes original posts with zero
  likes. It is `online only`; mutations touching it lose prediction.
  The seven reporting maintains are also `online only`.
- `snapback/reaction-cache.q` stores parallel arrays of principals and
  emoji, at most 50 each, in a second synced table. Its job scans messages
  50 at a time and reconstructs each cache; `chat.q` maintains it on writes.
  This cache carries individual reactors, not just counts: count chips
  replace its summary use, not an interface that lists every reactor.
- `evidence/m8-scale-backfill.txt` reports `insights.backfill` **done**,
  **1,047 chunks**, **366,624 rowsWritten**, `error: null`. This is physical
  row-write work, including derived writes, not 366,624 distinct source
  rows, and does not measure the separate reaction-cache backfill.
- `digestCounts` loops over up to 1,000 follows and 1,000 memberships,
  scanning up to 8,192 messages in each room. One profile per job chunk
  does not bound that profile's work. The shortened run's
  `digest-config.q` sets its period to 120,000 ms and anchor to zero.

The [R12 holistic note](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-14-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.12/astra/holistic-score.md)
charges “denormalization/backfill work for reporting and Trending, plus fewer
language escape routes”; its queries note also names the reaction cache.
The inventory records the September 5 Supabase scorer crediting
“Server-side counts, complete UTC day buckets, and transactional like counters
were natural extensions of the schema”. These are attributed builder/scorer
observations, not a comparative performance measurement.

The useful extension is a row's contribution to a maintain, followed by an
ordinary indexed read of its rows. It removes stored selection flags,
calendar buckets and summary caches. It does not derive an author's identity
through a post lookup, and it does not make a time-dependent feed into a
transaction-only calculation.

#### 20.9.2 One grammar, and its row shape

Proposed grammar below uses the existing Quarry lexical classes,
indentation, rule grammar and page grammar. `Name` may be module-qualified
where table names already may; `Column` and generated field names are local.
There is one `read` property, at most one `online only`, and any number of
distinct named indexes. No implicit filter alias or second group spelling.

```ebnf
Maintain    = Head NEWLINE INDENT Property+ DEDENT ;
Head        = "maintain" Name ":" ("count" Name | "sum" Name "of" Column)
              ["where" Predicate] "by" GroupKey {"," GroupKey} ;
Property    = "read" "<-" Rule NEWLINE | "online" "only" NEWLINE
            | "by" Column ":" Column {"," Column} NEWLINE ;
GroupKey    = Column | ("day" | "hour") "(" "." Column ["," Duration] ")"
            | "bucket" "(" "." Column "," Duration ["," Duration] ")" ;
Predicate   = Or ;
Or          = And {"or" And} ;
And         = Not {"and" Not} ;
Not         = "not" Not | "(" Predicate ")" | BoolLiteral | "." BoolColumn
            | Operand Compare Operand ;
Operand     = "." Column | ScalarLiteral ;
Compare     = "=" | "!=" | "<" | "<=" | ">" | ">=" ;
```

Only total, typed scalar comparisons enter a maintain predicate. Equality
and inequality admit null as the value model already does; ordered
comparisons require non-optional, comparable operands. A bare boolean column
must be non-optional. No row reads, `exists`, helpers, parameters, `viewer`,
`now`, arithmetic, JSON access or effect can occur here. Ordinary query
`where` keeps its broader grammar (§5.6); a maintain is evaluated without a
viewer and must remain correct when another table changes. This restriction
is checked at the offending expression, with a rewrite naming a stored
source field or the existing bounded job recipe.

**(a) Filter the contribution.** Examples in the proposed language:

```quarry
maintain originalPostCounts: count posts where .kind = 'original' by authorId
  read <- allow
maintain authorPosts: count posts where .parentId = null by authorId
  read <- allow
maintain visibleMessages: count messages where not .tombstone by conversationId
  read <- exists members[.conversationId, viewer]
```

The first is the brief's illustrative schema: `kind` distinguishes original
posts there. In the actual L12 schema `kind` is text/images/video and
`parentId = null` distinguishes originals; the second is its replacement
for `sum posts of original`. A filter also applies to `sum` with the same
typed contribution as today. It never changes the source's read rule.

**(b) Compute a key, without storing it on every source row.** Admit `day`,
`hour`, and, because the run actually uses two-minute digest periods,
`bucket` with a positive literal duration. Omit `week`: no M8 consumer needs
a week boundary, and there is no locale/ISO-week policy to invent here.

