```quarry
maintain dailyPosts: count posts where .parentId = null by authorId, day(.at)
  read <- .authorId = viewer
  online only
maintain hourlyMessages: count messages where not .tombstone by conversationId, hour(.at)
  read <- exists members[.conversationId, viewer]
maintain periodPosts: count posts where .parentId = null by authorId, bucket(.at, 2m)
  read <- allow
  online only
```

Each accepts a required `time` column, interpreted with its literal default
under §18.3. Output is an `int` ordinal: respectively
`floor(at / 86400000)`, `floor(at / 3600000)`, and
`floor(at / literal-duration-ms)`. Epoch is Unix UTC. Floor division is
Euclidean for negative timestamps: `day(-1 ms) = -1`, not zero. The output
fits the existing integer range for every admitted time value; no flooring
back to a millisecond timestamp can overflow at the negative endpoint.
Zero, negative and unrepresentable durations fail binding. No local timezone,
DST, wall clock or locale affects the key. Function,
input column, duration and literal offset participate in schema identity; a semantic change
rebuilds the maintain. The same functions are ordinary scalar builtins for
query arguments, so a daily scalar lookup uses `dailyPosts[viewer, day(now)]`.

**2026-09-15 — W5g calendar anchors (G7).** `day(time, 8h)`,
`hour(time, 30m)` and `bucket(time, 1d, 8h)` accept an optional literal
nonnegative offset strictly smaller than their period, in maintain keys and
ordinary expressions. The ordinal is `floor((time - offset) / period)`;
08:00 UTC starts the new day in the first and third examples. The same
integer key encoding and value forms apply on the server and both device
stores. A zero offset is omitted from serialized computed-key metadata;
old calendar calls keep their one-argument builtin IR, and nonzero offsets
use its existing argument list. Changing an offset rebuilds the maintain
without rewriting source rows. No local timezone or daylight-saving policy
is introduced, and join attribution remains a stored source field.

Computed columns on the target are named `day`, `hour` and `bucket`; bare
keys keep their source column names. Duplicate output names, including two
`day` calls or collision with `id`/`value`, are refused, never auto-renamed.
The target remains `{ id, <group columns>, value }`, with the canonical
ordered tuple as its existing maintained id. No value-model serialization
changes. `dailyPosts[viewer, day(now)]` is a scalar, zero for an absent group;
seven explicit scalar lookups still give seven days, including empty days.
An enumerated page lists existing groups only; it does not invent empty days.

**(c) Read a group as a bounded set.** An explicit page operator selects
row mode, using the existing `first`/`last`, `after`, index and shape syntax:

```ebnf
MaintainPage = Name ["[" PrefixArguments "]"] ["after" Expression]
               ("first" | "last") PositiveInteger "by" Column [Shape] ;
```

Without a page operator a maintain lookup requires its whole group key and
returns the scalar; a short key alone is a diagnostic, not an overloaded
array/scalar result. `byGroup` is generated over the complete group tuple.
An explicit page may pin any leading prefix, or scan globally with none.

```quarry
maintain reactionCounts: count reactions by messageId, emoji
  read <- allow
query chips(messageId: messages):
  return reactionCounts[messageId] first 50 by byGroup { emoji, count: .value }

maintain roomReactionCounts: count reactions by conversationId, messageId, emoji
  read <- exists members[.conversationId, viewer]
query roomChips(conversationId: conversations, messageId: messages):
  return roomReactionCounts[conversationId, messageId] first 50 by byGroup { emoji, count: .value }
```

The first needs a public source to inherit public sync. The second is L12's
membership audience and retains `conversationId` in the key; grouping by
`messageId` alone does not prove a conversation audience through a foreign
key. L12 already caps reactions at 50 per `(conversationId, messageId)`, so
there are at most 50 nonempty emoji groups. The binder propagates this
source cap as a bound on distinct target rows under that prefix; it adds no
runtime target cap and rejects no 51st distinct group independently of the
source constraint. Where no source cap proves the bound, `first 50` is just
a page: overflow has the ordinary cursor/completeness result. A finite enum
can also prove a distinct-key bound. The engine never silently truncates
chips and calls the set complete. Individual reactor lists and the viewer's
own reaction remain reads of `reactions` under its rule.
An expiring source's cap bounds live rows, not unswept physical candidates;
that proof never exempts its live group read from the meter.

**(d) Order by the maintained value.** Admit ordinary non-unique indexes on
target group fields, `value` and `id`, declared with the existing `by` line.
A value-order index consists of zero or more distinct group fields, `value`,
then `id`; its prefix determines a scoped ranking. No computed score or
cross-table order expression enters it. Other plain group indexes keep the
ordinary index rules. Examples:

```quarry
maintain postLikes: count likes by postId
  read <- allow
  by byCount: value, id
query mostLiked():
  return postLikes last 10 by byCount { postId, count: .value }

maintain authorPostLikes: count likes by authorId, postId
  read <- allow
  by byAuthorCount: authorId, value, id
query authorLeaders(authorId: principal):
  return authorPostLikes[authorId] last 10 by byAuthorCount { postId, count: .value }

maintain authorLikes: count likes by authorId
  read <- allow
  by byCount: value, id
query mostLikedAuthors():
  return authorLikes last 10 by byCount { authorId, count: .value }
```

`last` returns descending value and then descending canonical group id,
using the existing total key order on both stores. Ordinary cursors bind
the index, prefix and generation. Concurrent score changes can move rows
between pages, as with any mutable index; this is not a snapshot ranking.
Only existing nonzero groups participate (the existing zero-sum expiry
candidate exception is discussed below). Unliked posts do not acquire a
zero row just because `posts` contains them. `authorId` must still be a
validated field of `likes`; there is no automatic post-author join or
cascade when that attribution changes.

| Choice | Work and state | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Index every maintained value | Existing target rows and index insertion/removal; any N and prefix use the ordinary page reader | Recommend: the engine already maintains these indexes; only their authoring and row reads are missing |
| `maintain top N` | Must recover the next candidate after a winner decreases, moves or disappears; either keep the full value index anyway or rescan the losing groups; also needs membership transitions for each N | Do not add: an extra materialization and lifecycle for the same ordered read |

This ranks a maintained number, not relevance. It deliberately does not
claim to replace all of L12's Trending table: “original post created within
the last 24 hours, ordered by total likes, including zero-like posts” needs
post eligibility and time as well as a likes group. A query-time filter of
the first ten ranked groups is not its top ten. The exact existing
`ranked`/expiry recipe and LLP 3000.004's scheduled scoring remain the
forms for that request. A UTC-day group is not a rolling 24-hour window.

#### 20.9.3 Transactions, expiry, residency and disclosure

For an interpreted source image define its contribution as either absent
(predicate false), or `(computed tuple, count 1 / sum column)`. Insert adds
the new contribution; delete subtracts the old; update subtracts old and
adds new, coalescing equal keys before applying the result. False→false
writes no target row. False→true, true→false, and true→true with a changed
key have respectively one, one, and at most two affected groups. A change
to an unrelated field with the same contribution produces no target event.
Defaults are interpreted identically in ordinary writes, adoption, expiry,
sync and device execution. The source, target rows, value indexes and
partition events commit or roll back together. Replay applies once. A
target stays engine-owned; direct writes are refused.

Maintain totals use the current wide intermediate arithmetic and final
integer/decimal/money range check. A zero count removes its row; a zero sum
keeps today's semantics. Overflow refuses the source transaction with the
existing value-free maintain/column diagnostic. Maintains of maintains,
joins and custom reducers do not enter this design.

**Expiry is a live read, not a delayed truth.** **2026-09-25 amendment,
LLP 3000.013 Lane Q (0.4.2):** keep every selected physical contribution in
the stored total until its source is removed. The sweep subtracts it exactly
once. The internal contribution index is now `(computed group tuple, expiry
instant, source id)`, with null expiry ordered after every instant. At the
pinned read instant the server subtracts only the group's expired unswept
entries. Count subtracts one; sum subtracts the source value. A seek to the
first remaining live entry supplies the next invalidation and preserves
zero-sum candidates while any selected physical source remains. This is
constant work plus the unswept backlog, not the live population. Entries,
expiry-only moves and adoption backfill remain metered. A read-meter cut
returns no number, `complete: false`; a completed absent lookup remains zero.
The backlog is what the one-second, at-most-2,000-row, time-sliced server
sweep has not reached; there is no maximum age guarantee.

**Device stop accepted by the supervisor (2026-09-25, Lane Q review round 1).** The proposed delivery
of physical totals plus engine-owned next-expiry metadata is not implemented.
At time 100, compare an empty table with one selected physical row whose
expiry is 100. Both server live counts are zero and both current fresh
snapshots are empty. Delivering the latter world's physical total of one,
with its past deadline, would disclose an expired contribution to a device
that never held it. Hiding it from Quarry projections does not hide delivered
bytes from that device. The existing expired-row snapshot regression preserves
this boundary, so Lane Q retains the current live snapshot projection and
device recomputation rather than weakening disclosure.

Independently, the requested exact device answer after 300 expiries in a
10,000-row group conflicts with the specified existing recomputation rule:
the unchanged 8,192 examined-row ceiling cuts it. No larger meter or alternate
device algorithm is introduced. The server optimization and portable index
upgrade proceed independently; devices retain their existing costs and coverage.
An unavailable local read uses the existing server fallback; the server answers
within its backlog meter. This accepted behavior does not deliver physical
totals or add a device expiry certificate.

The round-1 adoption correction admits only plain indexes for cap validation.
Activation preflights serialized row lengths, reserves a whole fitting page
before fetching it, and retains that reservation through per-row contribution
evaluation. Pages shrink to leave decoding headroom inside the unchanged job
heap. Cap and uniqueness validation stream keys without materializing source
rows. A refusal rolls back rows, indexes, cap occupancy and metadata together.
PostgreSQL adoption likewise preflights a fitting serialized page under its
writer lock, then prices and releases each decoded row separately from that
resident page; the query scan’s conservative all-row decoder reservation is
not its adoption algorithm. Existence-only probes use the expiry component to
seek strictly past `now`, retaining the one-probe contract without walking the
expired prefix. Current device witness coverage still applies to each hit.

**2026-09-25, Lane Q review round 2:** the serialized-page allowance stays
reserved while a bounded admission pass checks each row's decoder and
contribution scratch. The decoder bound is charged once per decode; it already
includes its scratch multiplier. If the page and active row cannot fit,
adoption drops the page, halves its limit and retries before any callback
writes. Only a single row that cannot fit may refuse for heap. The admission
pass charges examined work, retains its meter across page-size retries and
accounts for optional/default fields without allocating them. SQLite and
PostgreSQL share this admission code; their fetched-buffer reservations remain
adapter-specific. A callback-boundary regression asserts that the entire
retained page and active row are charged together. Legal 1,000,000-byte bodies
and five 512 KiB bodies adopt; the SQLite five-by-1,280-KiB case specifically
requires a smaller page even with the corrected decoder charge.

**2026-09-25, Lane Q review round 3:** page admission fixes all charged
budgets before callbacks start. Each row includes one admission examination,
one callback decode and one examination per contribution index. Contribution
evaluation steps and the maximum selected index entries also limit page size;
the decoder and contribution heap allowances still coexist with the fetched
buffer. Every retry uses the remaining cumulative budget, preserving all work
from rejected attempts while releasing only dropped heap. A singleton that
cannot fit refuses before any callback; an empty range requires no row budget.
SQLite's 256-row, 63-view upgrade therefore splits into 252 and 4 rows instead
of refusing at examination 16,385. With twenty comparisons per view, the step
budget limits those pages to 198 and 58 rows. PostgreSQL applies the same
budget calculation to its per-index walk, including separate admission,
callback-decode and contribution examinations. The callback-boundary tests
assert cumulative counts and retained work across heap retries.

The predicate/key evaluator still serves writes, live recomputation, existence
probes and rebuilds. Existence-only rule probes retain their one-unit observable
charge. Value comparisons in rules over expiring maintains remain refused
under `E_QUARRY_LIVE_MAINTAIN_PROBE`.

**Refuse a value-order index over an expiring source in this slice.** Its
stored ordering can become wrong without a commit, including sums rising
when negative contributions expire. Rechecking only the ten winners cannot
find the missing winner. `check` names the source and rewrites to a live
bounded query or the scheduled ranked-table recipe; the implementation must
register the diagnostic on the expiry card. No sweep-lag assumption and no
exact time horizon maintenance are introduced to make the index look exact.

| Situation | Read and prediction contract |
| --- | --- |
| Source syncs, all audience columns survive as bare group keys | Inherit the resolved audience and **no target horizon**, as today; filtered rows reveal no new audience. Non-expiring maintained scalars and pages are `held`, even when source acquisition has a horizon |
| Public source, many groups | All inherited target rows reach every device; ranking `last 10` does not replicate only ten. `N_MAINTAIN_SYNCED` and measured residency show that cost |
| `online only`, or a source audience column is lost | Target is `server`; no automatic public projection of private votes. Queries and mutations reading it follow §6's server/queue-without-a-row contract |
| Predict a source change | Eligible only when ordinary source authority/old-image/absence checks and the affected aggregate base are locally decidable. A new eligible count, computed bucket, chip and index move use the same Rust contribution code. No rebuild from the device's partial source history |
| Expiring source not fully held | A live aggregate cannot trust its stored total; recompute held contributions and remain incomplete. A mutation depending on that live answer queues without a row |
| Source retained after leave | Preserve §18.2 for source history. Today a maintain inherits sync, not the source's `retain delivered-history`; its generated target defaults to until-revoked. Keep that rule in this slice, disclose it on the maintains/sync cards, and do not present a retained source page's length as its former total |
| Public source with subset sync under §20.7 | Do not infer that an aggregate over all public sources is complete inside a narrower delivery subset. Inherit only a statically source-complete group; otherwise classify the target read as server/unknown locally |

Merely maintaining an online-only target as an engine side effect must not
turn an otherwise predictable source mutation into a server mutation; the
device omits that target's overlay. Explicitly reading it, or writing an
online-only application table such as L12's `ranked`, does. An inherited
target with a locally unknown old value must never be initialized from zero
as if absence were proved. §20.5's separate upsert ruling governs an absent
application source row; this section does not bypass it.

**A filtered count can reveal a hidden field.** If a writer can change one
hidden row and read its count of `.kind = 'original'`, the before/after
delta reveals whether that predicate held. Computed keys reveal bucket
membership; rank exposes relative values. These are the declared aggregate
read channels of §8.7(g), not anonymization. Extend `N_MAINTAIN_DELTA` to
name filter/key dependencies and the readable target, conservatively when
source visibility is not proved for its aggregate audience, including
insert/delete channels. Keep the existing wider-update note. Recommend a
filtered example on `guide rules` that explains the disclosure and a
restrictive-read example that closes it. Inherited sync still requires
the source audience proof; an online-only aggregate's own read rule can
deliberately grant the channel. The filter grants no source-row read.

When the aggregate itself is hidden, filter-dependent work must not add a
new success/`E_BOUND` oracle on hidden-target writes. Extend the existing
schema-derived write-attempt reservation to the new predicate/key work,
up to two aggregate groups and their indexes, with heap based on declared
types; reserve before inspecting the hidden old image. Denied aggregate
reads keep the uniform-prefix/point-miss behavior, value-free errors,
dependency tables and replay withholding of §17. Paired worlds must test
this, not just compare returned counts. Existing §8 residual constraint
channels are not claimed solved. In particular, a filtered/keyed maintained
sum at an integer endpoint can refuse an admitted hidden write with
`E_OVERFLOW` when the hidden predicate holds and commit when it does not,
even if the aggregate's read rule denies the writer. The reservation bounds
work, not the stored sum. This is the constraint-outcome residual of §8/§17;
the refusal remains value-free and the failing transaction rolls back.
There is no paired-world success/error equality claim for overflow.

**Meter:** charge predicate nodes and key evaluation to steps, group/target
lookups to examined, actual index work (including contribution indexes) to
index entries, and each changed target row to rows written, alongside the
source charge. Reserve transient rows, keys and buffers before allocation;
release temporary heap when done. Group pages pay normal examined, rule,
heap and output charges. A value index does not scan the sources. Backfill
pays the same work under job ceilings per chunk, including excluded rows'
examined and predicate work. Hidden attempts use the conservative
reservation instead of a secret-dependent charge, never both. No ceiling
in `meter.rs` moves.

#### 20.9.4 Adoption owns the backfill

At base `56cdedba6a8ff343c8d297b246b7431824ef0241`,
`engine/write.rs::rebuild_maintain` scans in pages of 256 but holds all group
totals in a `BTreeMap`; `engine/mod.rs::activate` supplies
`Ceilings::unbounded()`. `store/activation.rs` publishes everything in one
SQLite transaction. Neither a page loop nor that transaction is a durable
continuation. The successor classifier does not yet describe filter/key
changes. Reuse its generation identity, interpreted images, constraint
validation and single publication boundary; add resumable preparation,
rather than describing the current rebuild as bounded.

Recommended first implementation, subject to the availability ruling below:

1. Classify a new maintain or changed filter, key function/duration, source
   default dependency or aggregate expression as a derived rebuild. A changed
   index alone rebuilds its index; a changed audience re-derives delivery.
   Report each step at its declaration. A source rename is still drop/add.
2. Acquire a durable store-wide write fence at the predecessor generation
   and sequence. Finish the current writer first. All application writers,
   jobs, seeds and expiry sweeps honor it; other processes cannot bypass it.
   Old-generation reads and logical expiry continue. A write not yet
   executed receives a retryable store-availability response and stays
   queued; it must not acquire an at-most-once failure receipt. Use a
   specific registered `E_ADOPT_BUSY` message, “schema adoption is preparing
   derived rows; retry this write with the same id”, family `store`.
3. Allocate candidate catalog identities, never reuse the live target's
   rows. Scan source by stable id into shadow target/contribution indexes
   in job-metered transactions, with a small page buffer and disk-backed
   per-group wide accumulators. Persist the candidate hash, predecessor
   generation/sequence, phase, source and last committed id with each
   chunk. Row contributions and cursor commit together. Do not keep an
   all-groups map or generate one huge partition event list.
4. Start with at most 256 sources a chunk, but stop before the next complete
   source's reserved work would exceed a job ceiling; commit the completed
   prefix. If one source and all its affected maintains cannot fit an empty
   chunk, refuse/park `E_BOUND` with the declaration and dimension, not a
   larger ceiling. Finalize wide sums and validate target rows/indexes in
   separate bounded phases. Do not reject a temporary out-of-range sum
   that cancels into range by the end of its group.
5. Recheck the predecessor identity under the publication transaction and
   swap candidate catalog mappings, backend and generation atomically.
   Devices use the existing generation replacement/acquisition protocol;
   they never observe a partially built maintain. Do not stream a single
   enormous rebuild commit. Obsolete internal rows are reclaimed in bounded
   cleanup chunks. Unchanged retained tables keep §18.2's handling.
6. On crash, recover the fence before accepting writers and resume the same
   candidate from its committed cursor. On overflow, invalid data or an
   unfit single source, cancel candidate publication, release the fence
   atomically and keep the old generation available; retain the private
   failure for `dev` to explain, then clean up. A cancelled fence invalidates
   its candidate for reuse because writes may resume. A different saved
   candidate invalidates the old preparation. No generic jobs framework,
   dual-write protocol or application backfill job is added.

Physical expired source rows belong to the same unswept contribution ledger
as ordinary writes; candidate live reads exclude them at read time. A clock
tick during preparation therefore needs no new time snapshot. Any legacy
expiry-candidate repair that this maintain needs joins the bounded phases,
not an unbounded follow-up inside publication. Mixed source projections,
constraints and index changes must also prepare before publication, or be
refused as an unsupported combined adoption; the old all-table activation
walk cannot remain hidden inside the final short transaction.

**`check` against the dev store.** Keep §18.3's read-only source/store
assessment and its unreadable-store exit 2. Show source rows to examine,
affected maintains/indexes, the job ceilings, and whether data assessment
finished; zero source-row rewrites are needed for these new derived forms.
Count scans are measurements of that store, not estimates of target groups.
An exact group count requires actually evaluating the keys. A full successful
assessment says “adopts after derived backfill”; it does not say the new rows
already exist. Invalid data or an overflowing final group gives the same
`E_ADOPT` in `check` and adoption with the maintain's location and rewrite.
No source with no store is presented as a populated-store assessment.

Large data-dependent assessments need a ruling against the one-second
`check` budget: recommend a bounded read-only assessment, exit 2 with
“derived backfill assessment incomplete; dev will prepare it in bounded
chunks” when it cannot finish. Never green on partial assessment, and no
mutation of the dev store to finish `check`. `dev` does the durable work
above and can still refuse it. This preserves “green implies the assessed
store fits” while making incomplete assessment an explicit third outcome.
The status wording/exit is proposed, not today's behavior; §18.3 must be
reconciled when the author rules. Structural resource certification is not
added: runtime meters still decide whether each chunk fits.

#### 20.9.5 Measurements and the implementation experiment

The opt-in measurement is
[`maintain_measurement.rs`](../snapback4/crates/snapback4-sim/tests/maintain_measurement.rs).
It uses the existing engine at the base above, debug profile, SQLite
`:memory:`, no sync or network, 1,000 groups, 50,000 post-shaped rows,
100,000 message-shaped rows, and a separate 100,000-row reaction workload.
Rows contain fixed small fields; they are not the full Clique fixture.
The reaction workload has 50,000 item ids and 100,000 distinct
`(groupId, itemId, emoji)` groups; it is a high-cardinality stress case, not
a claim about the measured run's reaction distribution. All ceilings remain
unchanged. Each mode seeds its own store, then measures adoption once and
200 inserts, 200 key/filter moves and 200 deletes. Timing is N=1 on the
shared loaded host, partly alongside the blocking suite; no disk durability,
replication or production latency claim follows.

`filter_proxy` is the shipped sum of a stored 0/1 flag, true on 75% of seed
rows; `key_proxy` adds a stored day ordinal. They measure current target-row
work, **not** proposed predicate/function execution. `chips` is the shipped
multi-column count. `rank_index` adds one plain `(value, id)` index to the
compiled target schema inside the test only, proving which existing engine
path can carry the proposal. It does not implement a new parser or reader.
The journal records exact measured numbers and the command transcript.

Measured adoption: count/filter-proxy/key-proxy took respectively
2,337.643 / 2,360.927 / 4,716.384 ms over 50,000 posts and
4,967.736 / 5,272.848 / 9,678.313 ms over 100,000 messages. The computed-key
proxy produced 37,500 / 75,000 target rows; simple counts produced 1,000.
The 100,000-group chip control took 12,757.363 ms to adopt; adding the value
index took 12,120.141 ms in its separate trial. That reversal is noise,
not evidence that an extra index speeds adoption. Chip insert/move/delete
p50 were 145.500 / 211.000 / 94.625 µs; with the value index,
158.917 / 227.125 / 96.333 µs. The final samples charged respectively
4/6/4 index entries versus 5/8/5, and 2/3/2 total rows written in both.
These moves delete and create groups; in-place numeric rank changes remain
in the implementation measurement plan. Existing meters do not charge
every internal aggregate lookup/allocation, so their printed totals are
observations of the base, not certification of the new evaluator.

Before claiming the implementation at the envelope, extend this measurement
with the real accepted forms, holding source/index/rule layout constant:

- Paired 0%, 50% and 100% filters; unrelated update, filter entry/exit,
  key move, last-source delete, and ranked winner decrease. Report all
  seven meter totals and actual target/index writes, plus p50/p95 time.
  Distinguish target index removal+insertion from a source row update.
- 50,000 posts and 100,000 messages from the scale fixture; 0/1/50
  reactions per message as separate cases, including the 5,000,000-row
  cap boundary case outside the fast suite. Measure group cardinality,
  rows/bytes acquired by a representative 50-member room and public reader,
  extra database/index bytes, peak temporary heap, total adoption time,
  worst chunk time, publication pause and durable chunk count. Use native
  SQLite on disk and both device stores, not only this memory control.
- Compare indexed top ten with an exact full-scan/sort oracle over the
  non-expiring groups. Measure rank reads as well as writes. No top-N
  operator is needed merely to compare its algorithm: deleting the winner
  must find rank eleven, so the full index is still the candidate source.
- Expiry: compare a group index with the old source-wide fallback, including
  a zero sum and a horizon device; record examined sources and incomplete
  answers, never extrapolate a complete answer from a partial scan.
- Adoption: inject restart before/after each phase's cursor commit, attempt
  a competing writer/adopter, and lower each applicable meter in the test
  to force multi-chunk and one-row-cannot-fit paths. Record the first
  successful chunk size instead of raising a ceiling. Run the failure
  histories through the simulator's seven invariants.

The index choice is based on reuse and deletion semantics; the measurements
establish its existing write path, not a speed comparison with an unbuilt
top-N operator. Durable write-pause length, new evaluator charges, contribution
index cost and bounded-adoption memory remain implementation measurements.
Do not accept a claimed complete scale implementation without those numbers.

#### 20.9.6 What stays out, and concrete acceptance

No arbitrary maintain expressions, cross-table joins, exact rolling-window
ranking, rank-by-relevance, time decay between commits, custom calendar,
empty-group synthesis, exact horizon eviction/refill, app SQL query surface,
JavaScript reducer, recursively maintained table, new jobs framework, meter
raise, value-model change or second language. Existing corpus cases stay
unchanged. These forms stay small enough for the card; README examples,
all cards and affected callers change only with their parser/runtime.

**S26 is only partly answered by buckets.** A digest can read per-author
period posts and per-author follower buckets without scanning their events.
“Unread since a member's moving read watermark, excluding their own and
tombstoned messages” depends on another row and cannot be a row-local
maintain predicate. The implementation must carry the existing bounded
receipt/unread-counter recipe or an application continuation keyed by
`(recipient, period, stage, room, source cursor)` across job chunks. Commit
partial totals and cursor together; emit and dedupe only after all rooms
finish. A heavy user spanning several chunks must finish or park with a
named refusal, never emit a capped total as exact. There is no claim here
that one user per chunk becomes safe, or that an arbitrary anchored digest
is a UTC-day bucket. A two-minute epoch-anchored period is supported by
`bucket(.at, 2m)`; another origin still needs an explicit source field.

The implementation lane's acceptance cases, alongside their positive forms:

1. **Language and cards:** compile/run the examples adapted to complete
   Clique schemas; keep full-key scalar lookup, add prefix row pages and
   global/scoped value order. Refuse a short scalar key, duplicate output
   name, optional-time key, zero bucket duration, viewer/clock/read/helper
   predicate, and missing rank prefix/index, each with a usable rewrite.
   Every new emitted `E_`/`N_` is in `guide::CODES`; every attached guide
   exists. All Quarry fences in README and cards compile without notes.
2. **Contribution truth table:** insert/delete and all four filter
   transitions, key moves, value changes, defaults absent in old images,
   no-op patches, grouped cancellation/overflow, unique-key upsert and
   duplicate mutation replay. Compare each commit to a from-scratch
   reference fold; an overflow rolls back source, target and indexes.
3. **Calendar:** −1/0/+1 ms, boundaries on both sides of a UTC day/hour,
   leap day, timestamps spanning a DST change, both integer endpoints,
   and two-minute buckets give identical native/wasm keys. Seven daily
   scalar reads return seven entries including zero days.
4. **Chips:** two principals on one emoji and one on another produce
   exactly two chips; an emoji change, unreact and last reaction deletion
   remove the right group. Exercise 50 reactions, refusal of the 51st
   source row, and an uncapped 51-group page with its cursor. A denied
   outsider cannot acquire the room's chips; a member can.
5. **Rank:** 11+ groups, ties, winner decrease, key move and deletion bring
   the right next group into top ten, globally and within an author.
   Compare to the oracle; test zero-group absence explicitly. A non-expiring
   count/sum index works; its expiring-source counterpart is refused, while
   the live unranked aggregate still works before and after a sweep.
6. **Device and disclosure:** run source/aggregate prediction on both
   device stores, including new proved groups, an unheld source, an
   unknown aggregate base, horizons, online-only side effects, membership
   leave and replacement snapshots. Preserve retained source history and
   revoke its until-revoked aggregate. Pair hidden/absent/filter-true/
   filter-false worlds near each meter limit and compare complete wire
   observations, including `tables`, errors, cursor, expiry, replay and
   cap metadata. A deliberately readable aggregate may reveal its declared
   delta; a hidden one may not gain a new filter-dependent budget channel.
7. **Adoption:** add then change filters, bucket durations, defaults and
   indexes on populated stores; no source backfill writes, exact first
   published totals. Crash/resume, failed final sum, one source too large,
   abandoned candidate, concurrent writer and replacement adopter leave
   one coherent generation; no candidate rows reach a device early.
   Read-only `check` and the completed adoption agree on data refusal.
8. **Digest and scale:** heavy recipient requires multiple chunks and emits
   once after completion, including restart and quiet period; no partial
   digest emission. Record §20.9.5's remaining measurements. Rust cases
   belong in core/sim suites and `device/tests/device.rs` for both stores;
   client cursor/residency behavior belongs in `packages/snapback4/test/`.
   Each new behavior starts with a failing regression and ends green; run
   targeted suites, `bash snapback4/scripts/test.sh`, and root `ref-check`.

**2026-09-14 — built by lane D1 (pending lead landing).** The implementation
now accepts row-local filters, day/hour/literal-duration bucket keys, scalar
and group-page reads, and scoped or global non-expiring value indexes.
Writes, live expiry reads, rebuilds and both device stores share the
contribution evaluator; expiring computed groups have an internal
contribution index. Source caps prove group bounds without a target cap.
Hidden attempts reserve aggregate work before inspecting the old image;
online-only side effects are omitted from prediction, and inherited targets
remain until-revoked. The cards and README describe the forms and disclosure.
Successor assessment names changed derived definitions at their declarations,
checks final sums, and uses today's atomic activation on populated stores.
The adoption lane still owns §20.9.4's durable preparation, write fence,
bounded chunks, recovery, incomplete-check result and bounded-memory scale
qualification; D1 does not claim any of those. S26's digest continuation is
also unchanged. Measurements and regression results are in the dated D1
entry of snapback4/JOURNAL.md.

**2026-09-14 — D1R1 review repairs.** Zero-sum prediction requires a positive
source witness or complete held coverage; incomplete live scalar misses and
pages remain unknown. Every expiring maintain has a selected contribution
index with a one-entry occupancy lookup prepaid with its work and scratch.
Public-subset aggregate reads are conservatively server-class. Core creation
and adoption reject expiring value indexes. Direct maintain and table page
cursors bind evaluated prefixes and active generations while remaining valid
across ordinary writes; rows can move between pages. The D1R1 journal entry
records base reproductions, independent scan/allocation measurements, both-store
source folds, index and partition-event checks, and suite results.

**2026-09-14 — D1R2, Grok review repairs.** Hidden deletes now reserve the
same schema-derived maintain work before fetching the old image and restore
the attempt's scratch/prepayment afterward. Paired observations cover both
filters, present/absent and allowed/denied targets, live/expired sources and
all seven meter ceilings. The hidden filtered/keyed overflow pair records the
§8/§17 constraint residual above. MemoryRows counts changed interpreted index
keys, including deletions; adoption tests fold targets after each changed
filter/key. The expiry card was already repaired by D1R1. The D1R2 journal
entry records the starting-HEAD reproductions and red/green measurements.

**2026-09-15 — W5e, G5/M2/S25: scheduled Trending keeps likes predictable.**
The value-index refusal remains. Physical maintained values and index keys
change together on writes and on the expiry sweep; logical reads recompute
live contributions before that sweep. The reader selects candidates and
continuation keys in physical order before recomputing values. A count's
stored winner can lose to an unvisited group at expiry, and a signed sum
can rise when a negative contribution expires. Winner-only filtering does
not repair either the top page or its cursor. A full live fold/sort is the
existing bounded-query alternative, incomplete at the meter; making a
sweep mandatory before reads would require a new server/device reindexing
and coverage contract. No such mechanism is claimed by this index extension.

The commissioned fallback is on `guide feeds`: non-expiring per-post like
counts, with `like`/`unlike` changing only the viewer's source row and
`create` changing only its post. A one-minute schedule writes the public
`ranked` table in 100-post chunks; application writes to ranked are denied.
Source writes remain eligible to predict, and both device stores exercise
their overlays and authoritative settlement. The online-only maintain is
an engine side effect, not a source mutation's server dependency.

The recipe ranks posts younger than 24 hours by like count, post time and
id, including zero-like posts. It is explicitly a post-time window, not
likes in a trailing window on arbitrarily old posts. Scores and new
candidates lag by one schedule period when execution is prompt; queue,
chunk and sync delay add latency. Each chunk reads current counts, so a
pass is not an atomic ranking snapshot. Expiring candidates at post time
plus 24 hours removes them on every host before any sweep. `complete`
describes page coverage, not score freshness. The diagnostic no longer
calls the scheduled recipe exact rolling-window Trending; it names the
predictable source write, the cadence and the feeds card. The immediate
bounded alternative remains available as `trendingNow` on the same card.

#### 20.9.7 Questions for the author

1. **Accept value-index ranking for non-expiring groups while exact
   trailing-day Trending, zero-like candidates and join attribution keep
   the existing recipe?** Recommend yes. Solving those with this maintain
   would require a projection/join or time-membership design and would not
   be the small index extension measured here. S25 is narrower than a
   promise to delete every `ranked` table.
2. **Permit a resumable adoption to pause writes while old-generation reads
   continue?** Recommend yes for this first implementation, with the
   measured disk-backed pause reported before landing. Preserving live
   writes would require an additional snapshot/delta-catch-up protocol;
   it is not already supplied by activation. No acceptable pause duration
   is established by this memory/debug measurement.
3. **May store-backed `check` exit 2 for an incomplete bounded derived-data
   assessment, leaving `dev` to finish preparation?** Recommend yes,
   preserving a green result's meaning and the short check budget. If not,
   choose explicitly between a potentially multi-second exhaustive check
   and a narrower first implementation that excludes data-dependent sums.
4. **Keep the current until-revoked policy on inherited aggregate targets,
   even when the source retains history?** Recommend yes in this slice,
   with the difference stated on the cards. A later explicit retain form
   needs to say whether the kept number is a last-delivered fact or a
   count of the retained source subset, and how redefined aggregates
   survive a new generation; those are different answers.

**2026-09-15 — the blind reviews of lane D1 and their dispositions.** Records:
`llp/reviews/3000.007-maintains.*`. GPT on `b8ca38bbf`: NOT READY — a
horizon device could delete a zero-sum candidate from partial history (High),
the hidden-write prepayment exempted an unbounded occupancy scan (High), subset
inheritance read falsely complete (High), core accepted expiring value indexes
(Medium), cursors bound neither prefix nor generation (Medium) → lane D1R1.
Grok on `b8ca38bbf`: READY WITH CHANGES — a hidden delete inspected the old
image before reserving maintain work (High), the expire card, hidden overflow
(documented as a residual), `MemoryRows` counting, the adoption fold → lane
D1R2. Lane D1I merged 0.2.16 without changing an inherited expectation. Both
families' narrow re-checks of `69bb3bfed` returned READY. Ships as 0.2.17.
### 20.10 A published simulator witness (S30)

**2026-09-14 — lane W1, commissioned by the author.** The native witness
capability is an app-builder surface: `snapback4 witness ios` in the binary,
`snapback4-expo/witness` in the Expo package. It replaces the repository-only
Metro witness. The deterministic simulator remains the primary harness;
this tool covers installed native process, durable storage and visible UI
boundaries that the deterministic engine harness cannot reach.

The driver builds nothing. It selects a booted iOS simulator by explicit
UDID, or requires exactly one available booted iOS simulator, verifies the
installed app and its Info.plist, and starts its own `dev --no-watch` in the
project. An already bound port is refused. `--prime` launches the app online
with segment `prime` until `primed`, then terminates it. The driver stops
its backend, verifies TCP connection refusal, starts video and device logs,
and launches the app cold with the requested segment. For `--relaunch` it
waits for `relaunch`, terminates the app, then relaunches the same segment
still offline. After `offline-done`, it restarts the same command on the
same port and project store, signals reconnect, and waits for `done`.
Each wait defaults to 120 seconds (`--timeout` overrides it). Only child
processes it started are signaled; every simulator command names its UDID.
An already running app is refused with `E_WITNESS_APP` and an instruction to
quit it; termination targets only the PID reported by the driver's launch,
leaving a replacement instance running.

The protocol is run-specific container files. The driver passes JSON in
`SIMCTL_CHILD_SNAPBACK4_WITNESS`; the native bridge reads the resulting
process environment only on an iOS simulator. Hermes cannot read runtime
launch environment through Expo's build-time `process.env` replacement, so
the bridge adds that constant and two small file operations. The helper
returns null without this environment, including ordinary subsequent
launches with old witness files still present. JavaScript owns the rest:
`segment`, sequential `step(name, fn)`, `receipt(object)` and
`waitFor(label)`. A successful step appends an empty application object
with `witness: { at, step }`; receipts inside its function use
`step: "name:running"`, and a thrown assertion emits `name:failed` and
rethrows. No application field is changed; the `witness` key is reserved.
Receipt append is synchronized to the Documents file before returning.
`waitFor('reconnect')` polls a file the driver creates only after its
restarted backend announces readiness. Files work without simulator network
entitlements or a separate control server. App assertions decide which
history, media, search, pending, settlement or recipient states to record.

The driver finalizes video before stopping logs, terminates the launched
app, copies the JSONL from its data container, and writes the run manifest
with installed build identifiers, simulator/runtime, backend command,
origin/port, millisecond start/end and lifecycle events, byte sizes, SHA-256
of video and receipts, and exit status. Missing/empty artifacts, malformed
JSON lines, failed or out-of-order steps, timeouts, and backend stop/restart
failures are coded, nonzero exits. A failed run retains available evidence
and a failed manifest; a retry cannot overwrite it. After output reservation,
preparation failures (including simulator discovery timeouts) also write a
manifest with `phase: preparation`, the error and exit status, without claiming
the app or backend started. Invalid input/platform and output-reservation
refusals cannot create that manifest. The reference wrapper
runs the published verb for both cold-offline and durable-relaunch scenarios,
and its chat app uses the published helper without a gated fetch or simulated
client reopen.

The manifest binds artifacts, not assertion truth. This does not establish
physical-device behavior, loss of every network path, read-receipt delivery,
media retention, search or view-once exclusion without the app's own asserted
and rendered observations. A mocked-simctl test is driver evidence only;
a Release app run on an actual dedicated simulator is the native witness.

**2026-09-14 — native runs and the ownership residual (lead).** The iOS
simulator runtime has no `kill`: the driver confirms through `launchctl list`
that the running instance is the PID it launched and then calls
`simctl terminate <udid> <bundle>`. A replacement instance that appears in
the instant between that check and the terminate would be stopped; the
simulator offers no PID-targeted terminate, and the witness runs on a
dedicated simulator, so the window is accepted and stated rather than closed.
An already-running or already-replaced instance is refused with
`E_WITNESS_APP`. Release simulator builds exclude x86_64 (the device
xcframework has no Intel simulator slice). Native end-to-end runs of both
segments passed on `f618456c5` and on `e01cc8afa`.

Both families reviewed the integration `0e9fdddba` (lanes A1, A2, B1, B2, C,
E, F, S over `a300ca605`) blind; each re-check read only its own family's
earlier output. Records: `llp/reviews/3000.007-agent-ergonomics-wave-2.*`.
Grok's headless plan mode ends on a narration-only message, so its sessions
were resumed with `--continue` until a verdict; the markers are kept.

| Round | Commit | GPT | Grok | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `0e9fdddba` | NOT READY: an upsert patch could change the insert image's audience (1); hidden secondary unique and cap populations (2); a merge continuation could skip a readable row (3); exit flush reordered writes (4); the left-group guard was not wired into prediction (5); publish could pack unverified bytes (6); a hold release lost a firing after a schedule edit (7); enum widening ignored text's control characters (8); `init` wrote package.json non-atomically (9); the writes card (10) | READY WITH CHANGES: left group (1), secondary unique (2), writes card (3), caps and `after insert` (4), a reused loop binding (5) | W2R1u (1, 2, 5, 10; Grok 1–3 and caps), W2R1m (3), W2R1c (4, 6–9); Grok 5 not reproduced — loop bindings are renamed (guard test) |
| 2 | `bca8bfe70` (with lane W1) | NOT READY: 1–10 repaired; A a stalled exit send wedged flushing and close; B the witness terminated an app it did not start; C the witness passed despite a failed step, and a `toJSON` envelope; D artifact namespace | READY WITH CHANGES: a device `after insert` probe could refuse what the server commits | W2R2e (A), W2R2w (B, C, D), W2R2a (`after insert`); the lead: the simulator has no `kill` (`simctl terminate` after the PID check), native end-to-end rerun |
| 3 | `e01cc8afa` | NOT READY: A, C, D repaired; B's check-then-terminate window; an empty receipt after `done` rejected; a final-exists proof that exhausts the meter refused instead of queueing (all Medium) | READY | The lead after the three-round limit: empty receipts after `done` accepted (unit test); a device proof's `E_BOUND` is unknown and queues (`a_final_exists_proof_that_exhausts_the_meter_queues_instead_of_refusing`, red `E_BOUND` → `E_PREDICT`); B's window accepted and stated in §20.10 |

The wave ships as 0.2.16 with lane W1's simulator witness (§20.10). Lane D1
(§20.9) and lane X1 (store replacement, §18.2 note) are under their own

### 20.12 The blind reviews of wave 3, and what was descoped

Wave 3 answered the 0.2.16 Astra calibration (holistic 90; record
`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-15-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.16`): lanes W3a
(web exit retry, replay before the first sync), W3a2 (a pre-admission
keepalive batch), W3b and W3b2 (feeds guidance, the tombstone and purge
recipe), W3c (papercuts), W3d (the development clock), W3e (online
`session()` parity). Both families reviewed the integration `34c4bb9d0`
blind; each re-check read only its own family's earlier output. Records:
`llp/reviews/3000.007-agent-ergonomics-wave-3.*`.

| Round | Commit | GPT | Grok | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `34c4bb9d0` | NOT READY: a meter-cut tombstone lookup exposes a removed event (1, High); a failed admission drops an outstanding exit predecessor (2, High); batch writes ignore the dev clock (3); a schema cap suppressed `N_NO_CURSOR` above the read ceiling (4); `init`'s ESM marker could reinterpret existing JavaScript (5); Chromium after-result unrecorded (6) | READY WITH CHANGES: tombstone insert authority was any viewer (1, High); W3a2 Chromium result unrecorded (2); the language card's `following` did not hide removals (3); grouped prediction notes listed IR sites (4); §20.4 behind the contract (5) | W3R1f (1: an engine-wide "a cut lookup is unknown"), W3R1a (2), W3R1c (3–5), the lead (6; Grok 2), W3R2 (Grok 1, 3–5) |
| 2 | `e41a564ab` | NOT READY: 1–6 repaired; A read-rule probe cuts bypass the unknown rule (High); B a confirmed replay can lose its settlement (High); C a permanent unrecorded refusal strands successors; D a heap cut after another cut refuses | READY | W3R3e (A, D), W3R3c (B, C) |
| 3 (confirmation) | `69fc90316` | NOT READY: A–D repaired; N1 non-uniform scans now distinguish hidden and absent worlds (High, a new disclosure); N2 a schema-adoption race permanently refuses valid arguments; N3 a throwing settlement listener duplicates notifications | — | The lead, at the three-round limit: descoped (below) |

**The descope.** Each round closed its findings and opened new ones in the
same two mechanisms: the engine-wide unknown-lookup rule (W3R1f, W3R3e) and
the pre-admission exit batch with its ordering obligations and terminal
receipts (W3a2, W3R1a, W3R3c). Neither converged, and N1 is a disclosure
the wave did not have before. Those five commits are reverted; everything
else in wave 3 ships as 0.2.19. GPT findings 2 and 3 and round-2 B and C
have no subject once the batch route and pending admissions are gone. N1–N3
leave with the code that introduced them. Both residuals were then
addressed after 0.2.19: navigation by W4a (§20.4), the cut lookup by W4b:

- **Unawaited navigation (§20.4, subsequently addressed by W4a's bounded
  synchronous journal).** At 0.2.19, a write whose `mutate` is not awaited
  before same-turn navigation can be lost if the page unloads before its
  IndexedDB admission commits. Real Chromium passes 8 of 10
  `witness/navigation-immediate.mjs` cases (the two unawaited zero-delay
  cases fail); the awaited `mutate` is the documented boundary.
- **A cut tombstone lookup (GPT 1), closed by W4b (2026-09-15).**
  Following now returns `merge feed after c last 20 by byReaderTime where
  not exists removedEvents[eventId = .eventId]` with the same inbox and
  broadcast lanes. The parser and binder admit `where` after a merge
  header's index and lower its predicate to each lane's existing scan
  filter. Each lane binds `.` to its row; named-table compatibility,
  matching-index rules, predicate typing and read rules remain unchanged.
  The scan already checks the meter after evaluating the predicate and
  before pushing its row. A cut public tombstone probe therefore stops
  before the unchecked event, while already checked live rows may remain;
  the existing merge rule withholds `next` on every meter cut (until wave 13's
  drain-cut exception, §20.22.5). No engine,
  rule-probe or meter semantics changed. Round-2 A remains unchanged.
  Removal authority remains owner-only (W3R2).

  The cursor query fills 20 live matches or reaches the end under budget,
  with no null slots, and continues after its last live result. Filtering
  costs work proportional to examined candidates, including removed ones
  and lookahead: the 40-event fixture with its newest 20 removed uses 82
  examinations and 41 predicate probes for its refilled first page. At
  ceiling 25 all three hosts return zero removed events, no cursor and
  `complete: false`; every examined ceiling from 1 through 82 does the
  same exclusion. A filtered merge without `after c` keeps the existing
  incomplete word when more matches exist. The hidden/absent non-uniform
  probing-rule scan, including a zero rule-probe ceiling, stays identical
  before and after across that same examined sweep on all three hosts.

  **W4b blind review round 1, lane W4R1b (2026-09-15).** A merge-header
  predicate that reads its discovery binding reproduced a wrong page on
  the server and both device stores: lazy discovery froze each prefix but
  left the final selector in the shared environment. The binder now puts
  the selector binding in the existing lane setup for every filtered
  discovery lane. That setup selects the eager path and evaluates each
  lane before moving to the next selector, including predicates expanded
  through helpers. This deliberately conservative fallback avoids a new
  dependency analysis or per-lane runtime environment. Fixed-prefix uniform
  filtered lanes on one seekable plain index remain lazy; unfiltered
  discovery lanes remain lazy. Following's private inbox plus named public
  broadcast remains eager with or without live pulls. No engine, point-read,
  read-rule probe, cut or serialized-value semantics changed.

  The selector regression returns exactly 48 matching rows in three pages
  of 20, 20 and 8, equal to both forced eager execution and an equivalent
  fixed-prefix lazy merge on all three hosts. The unfiltered control
  returns all 96 rows in five pages. Tests now pin the ordinary removal
  fixture at **82 examinations / 41 tombstone probes**, and ceiling 25 at
  empty data, no cursor and incomplete. A second fixture really promotes
  the five-follower author on follower 51, retaining old pushed tombstones
  and adding a live broadcast returned on the first page. It costs **85
  examinations / 42 probes**. Sweeps 1..=82 and 1..=85 return no removed
  ID or body and no cut cursor on server, memory and SQLite. Simply
  deleting newly shared posts would remove their broadcasts immediately
  and fail to exercise the long tombstone run.

  The N1 wire comparison now also reads `exists` on the non-uniform-rule
  table inside a merge predicate. Four forms, two probe ceilings and 82
  examined ceilings give **656 identical hidden/absent pairs per host**,
  with visible-row positive controls. A heap ceiling of **20 bytes** cuts
  while allocating the second tombstone key: **26 bytes charged, 3
  examinations, 1 completed probe**, null data, incomplete and no cursor;
  ordinary budgets return 20 live rows and a continuation. The cost, cut,
  hidden/absent and heap checks also pass against the base implementation
  `b1b9fd9ec364fcebd74caf20f533759ae9a99521`; these findings needed coverage
  and wording, while the selector binding needed a code fix. The feeds
  card and README now say “82 examinations”; the language card explicitly
  labels its body predicate a syntax example and points to the removal
  recipe on the feeds card.

W4a's synchronous pre-admission journal (§20.4) adds no transport and no
receipt: a journaled write reaches the server only through ordinary outbox
admission, so the reverted design's ordering and settlement obligations do
not arise.

**2026-09-15 — W6e: effect leases survive explicit development advances.**
W3d (`91d4ca9b184ff07de268d7eaae638b6a3b422e4f`) moved both effect claims
and lease sweeps onto the development clock; this predates W5g's presence
change. An explicit advance now moves every running effect's `lease_until`
by the same duration, under the engine and clock locks before exposing the
new time. The remaining real-time lifetime is preserved, including for an
already overdue lease; a failed lease update leaves the clock unchanged.
Retry `next_at` remains on simulated time. Ordinary ticks and `serve` keep
their existing semantics. W6e initially reported every E_EFFECT_LEASE at
settlement as information naming expiry and requeue; W6R1c corrects this below.
The jobs card warns that offset-stamped rows keep their times after restart
and recommends a fresh store per offset test (0.2.21 V4, H8).

**2026-09-15 — W6R1c: lost settlements report the recorded outcome; action claims survive advances.**
`runWorker` includes the server's code and message when an effect settlement
loses its lease. Only the server's recorded `queued` refusal is informational
and says requeued; `sent`, `running`, `failed` and unknown outcomes still go
to `onError` (or stderr). If a handler threw and its settlement cannot be
recorded, its original error is reported separately. An explicit development
advance shifts held action leases and their delegated viewer sessions with
effect leases in one transaction before publishing the new clock. Ordinary
sessions and retry times stay put; overdue leases stay overdue and revoked
sessions are not recreated. `serve` retains its existing clock and expiry.

### 20.13 Wave 4: the two residuals redesigned, and their blind reviews

After 0.2.19, two lanes redesigned the §20.12 residuals narrowly. Neither brings back a reverted mechanism.

- **W4a** (§20.4) journals each web admission synchronously in localStorage before `mutate` returns. The next open admits journaled records into the IndexedDB outbox. There is no new transport, no server route and no receipt. Real Chromium `witness/navigation-immediate.mjs` passes 10 of 10; it was 8 of 10 at 0.2.19.
- **W4b** (§20.12) adds `where` to a merge header, lowered into each lane's existing metered filter. Following checks tombstones before emitting an entry. No removed id or body appears at any examined ceiling, on the server and both device stores, and the hidden/absent sweep is identical before and after.

Both families reviewed integration `b1b9fd9ec` blind; each re-check read only its own family's earlier output. Records: `llp/reviews/3000.007-agent-ergonomics-wave-4.*`.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

### 20.14 Wave 5: fixes from the 0.2.19 calibration, and their blind reviews

The fourth Astra-medium calibration, on 0.2.19, scored holistic 91 (0.2.16: 90). Its friction review, `benchmarks/runs/2026-09-15-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.19/friction-review.md`, ranked the remaining Snapback-attributable friction. Wave 5 answers it with seven lanes.

**W5a** (G2, M4, M5, G12)
- `dev --replay` suppresses effect emission: suppressed effects are counted and never become claimable, across restarts, and schedules are held.
- `serve` and releases refuse the flag (`E_REPLAY_DEV_ONLY`) and never honor suppression.
- The cards say `--hold-effects` keeps effects claimable.
- README fixes: a start-here index; the sentence about the reverted batch route; normalized SecureStore keys.

**W5b** (d-06, M1)
- An insert arm that cannot satisfy §20.5 reports "updates only" and names the first failing condition, both in the summary and in one residency line.
- A device `insert … if absent` now needs the same absence proof as `upsert`, instead of predicting an insert it cannot prove.
- The prediction card covers membership-scoped reactions.

**W5c** (G6, G5)
- `client.acquired` and `useAcquired()`; a write queued before acquisition says so.
- `client.write(id)` and `usePendingWrite` track a write to its terminal receipt, using one durable receipt window of 256 per device store.
- The Expo starter opener awaits sync when online, and a network failure never blocks opening.

**W5d** (G3)
- `init --recipe feeds` writes the tested Following recipe after compiling it together with the existing schema, and refuses name clashes.
- `N_WIDE_MERGE` on a relationship-driven merge, and `N_SPARSE_FILTER`, name the cost and the command.

**W5e** (G5, M2)
- A scheduled Trending recipe keeps `like` and `create` predictable. It scores from non-expiring counts and post time, lags by one period, and ranks likes on posts younger than 24 hours.
- A value-order index over an expiring source stays refused, because the reader orders candidates before recomputing live values.

**W5f** (G4, G11)
- Page modifiers may appear in any order.
- A declared shape after a scalar `where` tail shapes the page.
- A single full unique index wins a positional key, unless an exact-type candidate already does.
- `?.` is repaired to plain field access.
- A modifier that could belong to either the inner or the outer read is refused with `E_QUARRY_MODIFIER_SCOPE` and a `--fix`; it never rebinds silently.

**W5g** (G7, G10, M8, M9)
- `clock advance` expires presence, and the expire, jobs, effects and presence cards point tests at the dev clock.
- The Expo starter's Metro `blockList` excludes the local store.
- Calendar maintain keys take a UTC offset (`day(.at, 8h)`); the zero-offset serialized form is unchanged.

### 20.15 Wave 6: fixes from the 0.2.21 calibration, and their blind reviews

The fifth Astra-medium calibration on 0.2.21 scored holistic 88 (0.2.19: 91). Its friction review, `benchmarks/runs/2026-09-15-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.21/friction-review.md`, found no regression from waves 4–5. The drops trace to three kinds of cause. First, unchanged items the builder surfaced or declined again: the password minimum counted bytes (authorization); the Following recipe could not be installed beside an app's own tables, so the builder rebuilt a global-timeline filter (queries_and_live, scale). Second, "predicts: eligible" hid main-path horizon guards (diagnostics). Third, the builder's own intent ledger (writes), plus scorer variance.

Wave 6 answers these in five lanes:

- **W6a** `init --recipe feeds --prefix <name>` renames every recipe declaration through the AST, so the recipe installs beside an app's own `posts` and `follows`. Feed entries carry app post references; the wired example joins the app's media cards and bridges follows. `N_SPARSE_FILTER` and `N_WIDE_MERGE` name the sparse-author condition and print the exact `--prefix` command. Installation refuses app declarations that would shadow a builtin or an unrenamed name the recipe uses.
- **W6b** The built-in password minimum counts Unicode scalar values; the 1,024-byte maximum is unchanged. The cards point password-only products at `use identity password`.
- **W6c** Prediction output keeps horizon guards visible:
  - `why` headings read "eligible except past the horizon at …";
  - a persistent summary bucket lists them;
  - `N_HORIZON_GUARD` flags an argument-id existence guard, advising removal only when its refusal code names a duplicate or retry;
  - `N_NO_HORIZON` never recommends a horizon that would cost an eligible mutation its prediction (whole-partition proofs, live-aggregate sources, ascending or other-index reads); instead it names the mutations;
  - `dev --replay` announces itself at startup and `doctor` reports it.
- **W6d** `client.mutate(op, args, { id })` works on the online, web and native clients:
  - duplicate caller ids compare and admit atomically in the device before prediction;
  - a same-id retry applies once and returns the original outcome, and reusing an id with different input refuses (`E_WRITE_ID_REUSE`);
  - an unknown caller id survives a local prediction refusal and reaches server replay;
  - ids take the `mintId()` form, and arbitrary fixture strings still need an application ledger.
- **W6e** `clock advance` extends held effect leases, action leases and delegated sessions by the advanced duration, so a live worker's claim survives simulated time. Retry backoff still follows the dev clock. `runWorker` reports lease loss with the server's recorded outcome and keeps the handler's own error. The lease bug was introduced by W3d, not W5g.

### 20.16 Wave 7: fixes from the 0.2.22 calibration, and their blind reviews

The sixth Astra-medium calibration on 0.2.22 scored holistic 91 (0.2.21: 88), and the builder adopted every wave-6 fix. Its friction review (`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-15-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.22/friction-review.md`) named one wave-6 regression and a set of older gaps. Wave 7 answers them in four lanes:

- **W7a.** A prefixed recipe install emits its standalone operations as helpers, so no client-callable operation accepts a foreign or absent app post reference (the grader's d-03 ghost event; `E_OP` now). Promotion no longer refuses because one follower sits at its pull cap: that follower is skipped, and a saturated new follow refuses with `FEED_PULL_CAP`. The recipe's follows carry `at`.
- **W7b.** A push decided only by the presence grace is deferred durably to the grace deadline and delivered once, or recorded `satisfied` with `presence_reconnected` if the recipient returned (the grader's d-02). The dev clock moves the deadline.
- **W7c.** Dev-only read meters (`createClient({ meter: true })`, or `x-snapback-meter: 1`) report the engine's counters for a real account; `serve` and releases never do. `run --as` takes a principal id. The dev banner names the store (`store: in memory — dies with this process`, or its path), with `--memory` as an alias. Service tokens name their store, readiness is documented, and `aggregates` aliases the maintains card.
- **W7d.** A measured "search every chat I'm in" section covers 100, 500 and 1,000 memberships on the server and both device stores, and `N_WIDE_MERGE` prints that form. Generated word lanes accept the search's shared merge cursor, which the held form needs to continue past page one.

### 20.17 Wave 8: a cut read says so, and the dev loop stops hiding things

The seventh Astra-medium calibration, on 0.2.23, scored holistic 88 with a better grade than 0.2.22 (88 assertions pass, 0 fail; O1 80, O2 72, O4 50, O5 75). Its friction review cleared wave 7 of the run's only major defect and named the real gap: **a meter cut reached the client as an empty success**, indistinguishable from "no matches". Wave 8 answers that and three operational gaps.

- **W8a.** A read reports why it was cut, beside `complete`. An author can declare a query device-only (`query … held:`); serving it refuses with `E_QUERY_HELD` instead of answering from a cut server scan. This is the paved answer for the calibration's search, whose fixture makes every message match and spends the examined ceiling about five times before a readable row.
- **W8b.** `init --recipe feeds` prints the caps it writes and accepts sizing flags, so an app above the defaults is not patched by hand after a refusal mid-replay, and the feeds card carries a measured cold-acquisition row for a wired app (139,413 rows / 17.9 MB at the calibration's shape).
- **W8c.** A failed startup adoption repeats its reason on the first failing job or sweep, appears in `doctor`, and retries when the lock clears. `doctor` and `/effects` show deferred pushes with their deadline, and the cards name the dev clock as the way to close the window in a test. Loopback `dev` returns read meters by default.
- Wave 7's emission shape had broken the feature-gated **Postgres evaluation adapter**, which the default suite never compiles; it builds again.

**The disclosure ruling (the lead's, after round 1).** Publishing a cut reason would let a non-uniform server read disclose hidden-row populations: with identical visible rows, one store answers `cut: null` and another `cut: "meter"`. So:
- device reads and uniform server reads report the precise reason;
- a non-uniform server read reports `cut: null`, documented as "not disclosed here", with `complete: false` still truthful;
- the precise reason stays in the dev-only meter;
- `query … held:` with `E_QUERY_HELD` is the paved production answer for a query the server cannot complete.

Both families reviewed integration `cb98c5de3` blind; each re-check read only its own family's earlier output. Records: `llp/reviews/3000.007-agent-ergonomics-wave-8.*`.

| Round | Commit | GPT | Grok | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `cb98c5de3` | NOT READY: public cut reasons reveal hidden-row populations (1, High); an exhausted rule-probe budget erases a real cut, answering complete (2); persistent adoption failures flood background logs (3) | READY WITH CHANGES: `snapback4 test` fails every held query (1); the adoption log is unbounded after its first line (2); doctor's deferral line is two snapshots (3); an unused public presence getter (4) | W8R1a (GPT 1 under the ruling above, GPT 2), W8R1b (GPT 3; Grok 1–4) |
| 2 | `77e0dee25` | READY WITH CHANGES: 1 and 2 repaired with regressions that fail without them; 3 partially — two alternating reasons per code still print | READY | W8R2 (per-code rate limit: 160 alternating lines → 2, 158 suppressed) |

The lead then fixed a recurring full-suite failure that three earlier runs had treated as load flake: **orphan adoption skipped a tab that had just closed but still held its lock**, and nothing retried until a later apply, so a receipt that tab left was unreachable even after a sync. Adoption now waits briefly for that lock.

### 20.18 Wave 9: the docs and diagnostics catch up with the surface

The eighth Astra-medium calibration, on 0.2.24, scored holistic 88 with O2 75 and no failing assertions. Its friction review found **no wave-8 regression** and showed wave 8 paying where the builder found it: `query … held:` was adopted twice, M5 wall fell 827 → 609 s, and the 0.2.23 run's major defect now fails at compile time; sized caps meant the recipe was never hand-patched; the dev clock halved M6. But three wave-8 surfaces went unused — `Read.cut`, the default dev meter and `doctor` — because the builder never opened `guide reads`, having opened 11 of 27 cards. The run's own major defect was an app timing margin, not Snapback.

Wave 9 therefore spends itself on what Snapback tells a builder:

- **W9a.** Four statements the cards were missing: a live reader may drop an expiring row at its own `expiresAt`; a held query's proven property, stated positively; a wide-merge rewrite that names a command that exists; and that a read denied by a rule answers as an empty page, by design, so a hidden row cannot be probed.
- **W9b.** `why <CODE>` prints the card's section instead of pointing at the card, bounded and with the pointer retained. `N_MAINTAIN_SYNCED` stops printing a count that is structurally zero. Residency sites carry `file:line`.
- **W9c.** Two papercuts that had recurred four and two times: a bounded-list loop whose body only emits is no longer refused as structural fan-out, and `unique byHandle: handle else HANDLE_TAKEN` lets a constraint name its own refusal instead of surfacing storage-constraint text.

### 20.19 Wave 10: one way in, and a diagnostic that carries its own explanation

The ninth Astra-medium calibration, on 0.2.25, scored holistic **91** — the
program's best — with queries 78 → 86, scale 80 → 87, zero Snapback defects and
no failing assertions. Its friction review found no wave-9 regression. The
largest single scoring item was not a feature at all: the README had been split
in two (and then, by another session, into three), and the builder logged the
client reference as a separate lookup twice while the scorer named four places
to look. Wave 9's headline, `why <CODE>`, was invoked **zero** times, and
`guide reads` went unopened for a second consecutive run, so `Read.cut` and the
dev meter stayed unconsumed. Pointing at a card was not reaching the builder.

Wave 10 therefore spends itself on reach — on the distance between a fact
Snapback knows and the moment a builder reads it:

- **W10a.** The README's opening names all three documents and what each is for,
  with a "Start here" path; when a note or refusal repeats in one `check` run,
  its card section prints inline under the first occurrence rather than only a
  `guide:` pointer; `N_NO_CURSOR`'s rewrite names the `cap` remedy as well as the
  cursor; and the effect/job qualification asymmetry is documented where a module
  declares both.
- **W10b.** The installed recipe stops costing the same three corrections: the
  `feedRepost` parameter is renamed so the obvious call compiles, `--authors N`
  sizes the backfill literal with the caps instead of leaving it behind, the
  expiry filter is shown on the typing indicator where it actually bites, and
  `test --residency` is named at installation, once.
- **W10c.** Four recurring low items, each already paid for by more than one
  lane: `matching ''` matching nothing is documented with the paved shape, the
  one-time service token moves off the stream harnesses capture into committed
  logs, `N_NO_HORIZON`'s silence is stated as reader-dependent rather than
  approval, and a cascade diagnostic whose rewrite the builder cannot act on is
  suppressed when an earlier error on the same operation explains it — with a new
  `check --verbose` keeping it reachable and a count line so nothing vanishes
  silently.
