of merge so that another session's concurrent `routes` feature stayed out of the
diff. Records: `llp/reviews/3000.007-agent-ergonomics-wave-10.*`.

| Round | Commit | GPT | Second family | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `263495ceb` | READY WITH CHANGES: a job whose prelude parses but whose chunk body is empty had its empty-body error marked as a cascade, so the default `check` hid a diagnostic carrying an independent repair (1, Medium); `N_NO_HORIZON`'s new silence sentence inverted the rule stated two sentences below it (2, Medium) | NOT READY, 14 findings: the backfill raised to `min(authors, 1000)` exceeds the 1,024-row mutation ceiling on the recipe's own declared envelope (F1, High); `dev` never received the cascade suppression, so it still prints the contentless diagnostic the item was filed on and now disagrees with `check` about the problem count (F2, High); the inline excerpt prints a "no dedicated section" banner for the four commonest repeated codes (F3); `N_NO_HORIZON` (F4, = GPT 2); the `N_NO_CURSOR` cap remedy proposes a whole-table cap on an unscoped page and a *smaller* cap on an already-capped table (F5); backfill documented unconditionally in one place and conditionally 22 lines later (F6); `--authors < 200` silently delivers less history than before the wave (F7); the residency pointer is not runnable and `--fixture` fails open on a missing directory (F8); no test pins the merge ordering (F9); four Low; and F14, governance | W10R1 (GPT 1, 2 = F4; F9's ordering test), W10R1b (F1, F2, F3, F5, F6, F7, F8, F10–F13), lead (F14) |

**The two families agreed on one finding out of sixteen.** That is the case for
paying for both: each High finding was raised by exactly one family, and both were
real — F1 is a regression this wave introduced, and F2 is a fix that missed the
evidence it was filed against, since the friction item's transcript was `dev`'s
stderr and the suppression landed only in `check`.

**Two of this wave's own claims were false and are corrected rather than defended.**
The `N_NO_HORIZON` sentence said silence meant no reader had forced the check; the
wave's own regression fixture proves the opposite — the note fires *without* a
reader and falls silent *with* one. The `N_NO_CURSOR` cap remedy was sound only for
a scoped page; on an unscoped one it proposed a constraint that refuses the table's
next row, and on an already-capped table it proposed shrinking the cap below rows
that already exist.

**The 1,500-line rule is now about source, not the published documents**
(`snapback4/NOT-DOING.md`). The rule exists to keep a source file reviewable. This
wave exists because applying it to a document cost the 0.2.25 run its single
largest scoring item: the README was split, and the builder then had four places to
look. Splitting it again to satisfy the rule would undo the wave's headline. The
exception is narrow — the package's published README and its linked references —
and the census that enforces the rule covers source and says so. **This relaxes a
stated constraint and is flagged for the author.**

**One merge was the lead's, not a lane's.** W10a and W10c independently gave
`report_diagnostics` a third parameter with different meanings — `explain_repeats`
and `verbose`. They are merged into one function that suppresses redundant
cascades *first* and then counts repeats over only the surviving diagnostics, so
a hidden diagnostic cannot earn an inline excerpt; verbs other than `check`,
which expose no `--verbose` flag of their own, show every cascade rather than
hide one the caller cannot reveal. It was put to both reviewers as its own
question.

**The second family was not Grok.** For this wave the Grok CLI narrated one
sentence and exited on every headless invocation — both prompt forms, four
permission modes, on a one-step task, with no leader process to blame — having
worked normally six hours earlier. Rather than grind the wrapper, the second
review was taken from a Claude reviewer on the identical brief, read-only and
without sub-agents. The review records name the families that actually read the
diff. This is a weaker second opinion than the program's usual two independent
vendors, and it is recorded as such.

### 20.20 A fresh ranking is a `maintain`: the derived-write question is closed

The 0.2.21, 0.2.22, 0.2.23, 0.2.24, 0.2.25 and 0.2.26 reviews each carried a
proposal for engine-authored derived writes — a `system update` spelling, or a
privileged helper — so that a ranking could be fresh at load without a schedule's
lag. The 0.2.26 review framed the blocking question as an authorisation decision:
may an authorised source mutation cause a declared, deterministic derived-table
update in the same transaction under engine authority, while ordinary principal
writes to that target remain denied?

**That capability already ships, and is called `maintain`.** A maintain may be
indexed by its own `value` and grouped by a window column; put the window first in
the index and the ranking is scoped to it:

```quarry
maintain trendScore: count likes where .isOriginal = true by postDay, postId
  read <- allow
  by byScore: postDay, value, id
```

It is kept inside the source transaction, is server-run and so fresh at load, and
its target is refused to every principal at compile time. Both families verified
each of those independently. **No new authority is required and `system update`
should not be built**; the residual carried since §20.14 is closed rather than
deferred a seventh time.

Two corrections belong with the ruling, because the first published example of it
was wrong in both directions and the blind reviews caught both.

- A maintain's **target** cannot be written by a principal, but its **score is only
  as trustworthy as the source rows' insert rule.** The first example declared its
  source table `insert <- allow` with `principal` as an ordinary column, which let
  any caller forge contributions and choose the window bucket — a weaker position
  than the handwritten table it was recommended over. A denormalised fact must be
  derived from the authoritative row inside the mutation; a maintain cannot validate
  one.
- A `count` maintain has **no row for a group with zero contributions**, ties break
  by maintained id, a maintain target can neither expire nor be capped, and
  `day(.at)` is a calendar bucket rather than a rolling window. A trending feed
  usually wants a rolling window, so the maintain suits a current-bucket ranking and
  does not replace the handwritten recipe everywhere.

**The finding is not that Quarry lacked a feature — it is that six consecutive
reviews escalated a design decision for a capability that shipped, and two builders
in a row hand-rolled a less safe substitute that nothing refused.** That is the
programme's standing theme in its sharpest form, and it is why the missing card
sections of §20.19 matter more than another language feature.


### 20.21 Rolling rankings: live expiry, transactional sweeps and a fresh bounded path

**Revised:** 2026-09-17 — W12R1a corrects cut values, the candidate bound,
heap limits and the default sync horizon after W12a's outcome (b);
W12R1c (W14a) corrects this section's prescribed post-window
`trending()` read-time sort: a value-indexed count over non-expiring likes,
grouped by `hour(.postAt), postAt, postId`, merges the last 25 hours and
filters the exact trailing edge. The copied post time is checked against
the post by the insert rule; no principal writes the target. Membership
changes with the post's immutable time, while scores change only in source
transactions, so this ranks recent posts without scanning the whole window.
`rollingLikes()` and its live-expiry limits remain unchanged. Whole old
buckets are skipped, but rejected rows in the oldest included hour are
charged and can still cut the read; cursor pages are not a snapshot across
score changes. The card includes a bounded zero-like fill, public-sync cost
and measured persistent group lifetime. The earlier investigation and its
measurements below remain the record of W12a's bounded form.

**W12a investigation, with W12R1a/W12R1b corrections.** The bounded
path below records the earlier post-window form and the still-shipping
like-window form. The exact indexed post-window form above now ships;
the count upper-bound threshold read for expiring contributions remains
unbuilt. This closes the expressiveness investigation following §20.20 without
reopening derived-write authority. It does not claim an exact indexed top-N
across arbitrarily many expiring contributions.

**Corrected:** 2026-09-17 — W12R1b: the lead's claim that sound indexed top-N
requires all expired contributions to be swept was wrong for `count`. The
decision not to build that read form stands; it is unbuilt, not impossible.

**Investigation before implementation.** `Engine::sweep_expired` in
`crates/snapback4-core/src/engine/mod.rs` deletes source rows through
`Runner::remove` → `row_changed` → `maintain_one` → `apply_aggregate`.
The source deletion, physical aggregate subtraction, all changed target index
entries and partition events already commit in one transaction. The physical
contribution is retained until deletion and subtracted once. No separate
aggregate correction mechanism is missing from the sweep.

`Runner::live_aggregate_inner` in `engine/read.rs` instead recomputes a selected
group from live source images at the query's pinned clock. It ignores expired
contributions before physical cleanup, on the server and on both device stores.
Therefore a stored value index would order physical totals while returning live
values: stored order is stale. Taking top-N directly from that order is unsound
for every maintain kind, before cleanup or after a partial sweep. Rechecking
only stored winners cannot discover an omitted winner. Deferring truth until
the sweep would violate §20.9.3 and the existing expiry contract.

For `MaintainKind::Count`, however, `delta()` contributes ±1 per source row,
and the live read counts the same selected group minus expired contributions.
At one server snapshot and pinned clock, `stored(g) >= live(g)` always. A
descending stored-value index is therefore a sound upper bound: recompute each
candidate's live count, keep a top-N heap, and stop once the next stored count
is at most the N-th best live count. No unexamined group can beat that score;
if a deterministic secondary order is promised, equal-score candidates must
also be resolved by that order before stopping. Exhausting the candidates also
terminates exactly. This threshold scan needs no sweep, though it can examine
every group and must report incomplete if the meter cuts it before the proof
is established. That read form is **not built**.

The upper bound does not hold for signed `Sum`: expiring a negative contribution
can increase the live value above its stored value. Nor is it a device invariant:
snapshots deliver live aggregate values, not the server's physical totals.
A count threshold read based on physical upper bounds would be server-only and
would have to refuse on devices. The existing bounded live reads remain valid
on both device stores under their source-coverage contract.

**Cost is not the blocker.** The sweep uses a `Meter` with
`Ceilings::unbounded()`, not author/job ceilings. Its explicit bound is its
source-row `limit`, scanned in batches of at most 256; the host requests 2,000.
`apply_aggregate` already calls the store's ordinary indexed `put`/`delete`.
One value index adds two entry writes per changed surviving group (old key
removed, new key inserted), one on group deletion, and no additional target row
write, group lookup or source scan. A 2,000-source batch affecting one maintain
therefore adds at most 4,000 rank entry writes; several maintains/indexes multiply
that work. The paired store experiment measured 2,000 changing totals on SQLite and
MemoryRows: without `byValue`, insertion/change/deletion touched 2/0/2 entries;
with it, 3/4,000/3. The measured deltas are 4,000 rank entries across the
changes and one on final group deletion. These are index-operation counts, not a sweep wall-time
benchmark or a claim that an unbounded number of indexes fits a fixed budget.
No ceiling changed.

**Decision and built surface.** Outcome (a), merely admitting the index and
serving its stored top-N while maintaining it in the sweep, is unsound between
sweeps and after partial cleanup. A count upper-bound threshold read would need
a new read form and device refusal; this increment does not build it.
Outcome (c) is unnecessary for the bounded live case: no writable materialised
score or privileged helper is needed. The feeds card replaces its handwritten
fresh-ranking table and `refreshRank`/`rebuildRank` helpers with executable live
queries. The existing scheduled recipe remains an explicitly delayed option
for stored paging, not the freshness rewrite. The refusal now states the stale
stored-order failure and the unbuilt count threshold read, and routes by window intent:
hour lanes over non-expiring likes for the post window, or the live bounded
`rollingLikes()` recipe for the arrival window, with its cost.

The earlier bounded `trending()` scans posts in `(now - 24h, now]`, reads their live like counts,
sorts by descending score/time/post id, then takes ten, including zero-like
posts. `rollingLikes()` scans existing live count groups, sorts by descending
score/post id, then takes ten: recent likes on posts of any age, without
zero-like groups. Each like expires at its creation time plus 86,400,000 ms;
the declared mutation derives principal and times, accepts no score, and only
inserts/deletes the viewer's unique like. Source updates are denied and count
targets are compiler-protected. Repeated set/clear is idempotent; a repeated
live like does not extend its expiry. The default keeps likes private and
queries on the server, with `sync public last 2000 by byTime` on posts.
The documented public-like variant changes its audience, removes `online only`
on the maintain and explicitly removes the posts horizon; `N_MAINTAIN_SYNCED`
states its cost. It replicates full source and aggregate groups to compute on
either device.

**Limits and evidence.** Both bounded queries use `first 4095` and share the 8,192
examined ceiling with nested count and live-source reads. Each post costs two
examined units plus one per live contribution; unswept expired sources also
cost examinations. With short bodies and one like, 4,095 candidates return ten
complete winners at 8,191 examinations and 7,477,013 heap bytes. At 4,096,
lookahead spends one more examination; the selected 4,095 candidates still
have exact scores, but the ranked prefix is incomplete. The like-window query
pays one per count group plus its sources: 4,095 groups with one live like each
can fit too. Neither bound guarantees capacity with more contributions.

W12a's 8,194-candidate answer was not an uncertified ranking with correct
scores: its scan exhausted the meter before projection and the binder turned
unexamined point results into zero, omitting the only liked post. W12R1a chooses
an unavailable query over invented data. A maintained point lookup at a cut or
an unfinished live aggregate now unwinds the Quarry query to `data: null,
complete: false`, without a continuation; the missing-group zero is reserved
for an uncapped lookup. This also replaces the older partial-contribution
count result on maintained scans and points. Candidate-only truncation still
returns accurately scored prefix winners with `complete: false`. Tests assert
the winner and every returned score at 4,095, 4,096 and 8,194 candidates, and
assert unavailable results for a group whose 8,192 likes exhaust the meter.
No ceiling is raised and the serialized value model is unchanged.

The separate 16 MiB heap ceiling includes whole post bodies materialised before
projection. With 6,000-byte bodies, 1,000 candidates fit at 2,000 examinations
and 7,822,758 heap bytes; 2,700 cut at 2,700 examinations and 17,375,972 charged
heap bytes, returning `data: null, complete: false` before scoring. This is a
metered allocation charge, not a peak process-memory measurement. There is no
ranking cursor over the unsorted source, even for a candidate-only cut. These
bounded reads trade index lookup for read work; the general large rolling
index remains an expressiveness limit, now named rather than hidden behind lag.

The device witness uses stored counts 3 and 2, expires the first group's three
contributions, and immediately ranks the second group first without any server
sweep or sync. Three one-row sweeps reduce the first physical count to 2, 1,
then absent, while live ranking stays correct before and after each delivered
commit on SQLite and memory. It also checks exact post-window boundaries,
zero-like candidates and four immediate set/clear results. The server witness
checks two creates and seven set/clear writes with scores 0,1,1,2,1,1,0:
zero schedule runs and zero milliseconds of scheduled lag. Remote writes still
need sync; offline results describe held data. A source horizon makes local
ranking incomplete. Post-window bounds are reevaluated on query; a caller must
requery on clock advance for zero-like boundary changes, while live contribution
reads provide `nextExpiry`. No automatic exact time horizon is introduced.



**2026-09-18 — W15c, ruling 41's interim value-index tie-break.** The binder
admits a prefix of group fields, then `value`, then any remaining group fields,
then `id`, with no repeated field. When every group field is present, the author
may omit `id`; binding appends it, preserving the existing total-key and cursor
path. Thus the feeds recipe's `hour, value, postAt, postId` is admitted and equal
scores order newest-first; `hour, value, postAt` still refuses because `postId`
is missing. Store maintenance and merge continuation need no engine change.
The server and both device stores walk 48 equal-score rows in both directions,
including post-time ties; server drain cuts continue four times per direction
without loss or duplication. Two predicted source writes move a row out of and
back into the tied order on each device store. On base `0518ec9e761e114a02d7fc46e5520a102991c627`
and the candidate, the 48,200-post page examines 34 rows; the paired 37-post
paging fixture examines 33, with all seven meter counters unchanged, and
`check --cost` still reports 25 lanes. The brief's 265/276 and the card's older
264 are not this base's measured counts. The README now has a prose pointer,
not a twin Quarry block; that pointer and the feeds and maintains cards are
updated together. The maintains card says "until maintained columns ship", as
[ruling 41](#14) requires; the two-family blind review and landing remain the
lead's work. The expiring-source refusal and the unbuilt threshold read are
unchanged.

**2026-09-19 — W15b, ruling 31's provenance census.** The note now proves a
column from all Quarry write values across the catalog: table and maintain
reads (including prior-image arithmetic) propagate through bindings, calls,
objects, shapes and loops; an argument or ambient-only write defeats the proof,
except a literal insert/default may initialize a column with another read-derived
write. Read selectors do not taint the stored value; value-selecting conditionals
do. Unknown provenance, including a native job's unconstrained writes, is silent.
Recursive loop-carried JSON growth is summarized as an unknown subtree while
preserving its provenance bits and known sibling fields, so this analysis terminates.
The existing read-audience, unconditional-rule, immutable, append-only and
maintain-target checks remain. Before enabling the note or revising its claim,

the promotion allowance is 159 rather than 107 and the backfill is 865 rather

sites; six initial presence-only hits were removed by classifying `connected`
as ambient rather than a table read. Retained calibrations 9–12 emit 2/1/3/3

exposing its non-immutable copied `postId`. The feeds note section and README
are verbatim twins with a compiled pinning example, tested at zero and at the

**2026-09-19 — W15b round 2, bounded conservative provenance.** Abstract values
share subtrees but are limited to 4,096 expanded nodes and 128 levels; the entire
catalog shares 262,144 weighted work units and a 128-frame traversal limit.
An oversized value becomes UNKNOWN; exhausted traversal abandons every
all-writers proof because skipped writes could defeat earlier evidence. Branch
and loop control reaches helper writes and their cached write summaries, without
turning ambient values into read evidence; sort/group selection reaches nested
fields, and optional shapes retain source selection. The literal-insert identity
exception still applies under a branch. The audit adds silent regressions for
these paths, short-circuit writes, unknown tables and rule-only expressions.
Published material and the ten corpus sites are unchanged. The requested zero
calibration delta is incompatible with the control rule: all four retained
`feedPullCounts.used` columns and calibration 12's `posts.score` have writes
selected by boolean arguments and now stay silent. Calibration 11's
`trendingPosts.score` remains: only its literal initializer is argument-guarded;
its derived write remains read-controlled. Retained counts are 1/0/2/1, with no
name-based exception. The journal records the complete audit and measurements.

**2026-09-19 — W15b round 4, data dependence under [§21.8 L26](#21.8).**
The preceding lane records describe the superseded information-flow verdict.
The note now separates data from control: every non-initializing write must
have READ data (possibly AMBIENT), with neither ARGUMENT nor UNKNOWN data;
arguments may choose branch arms, loop counts/exits and sorted or sliced
picks without supplying the written value. Control does not decide the verdict

**2026-09-20 — W15b round 6, cardinality under [§21.8 L28](#21.8).**
L26's allowance for caller-selected values does not allow the caller to supply

cardinality separately from its items: READ for a read population, ARGUMENT for
an argument list or argument-bounded range, AMBIENT for a literal list. Shapes,
slices, sorts and groups preserve it; a filter may add its predicate's data.

consume it together with their data. Helper results, nested fields, collection

corpus **10 → 10**, retained calibrations **2/2/4/3 → 2/2/4/3**, with every exact
column set asserted. The feeds card and `why` now say that an argument may not
supply the value itself or how many values contribute to it. The sixth journal

**2026-09-20 — W15b round 7, loops prefer silence under [§21.8 L29](#21.8):**
at the loop join, every binding assigned anywhere in the body takes the domain's
cardinality into its data, including nested branches and loops, resets and
assignments before or after a break, so argument or unknown domains stay silent
and read-domain counts remain noted, with the superseded reset precision recorded
