acceptance exercise `test/first-account.test.mjs` assembles the first screen
from the README and the starter alone — signup and login, profile, avatar,
reopen with the session restored, a denied read — against the generated API on
the online tier and the SQLite device: red against the old README, green after.
No runtime, parser, client, wire, value-form, ceiling or diagnostic text
changed; `why` output is byte-identical. Recheck at 490ee9cfac: READY from both families, no findings. The prediction it carries is
pre-registered (`issues/20260919-pre-registered-predictions-for-calibration-15.md`):
`setup_and_docs` ≥ 91 confirms, ≤ 85 refutes, with the mechanism criterion (the
lines a cold builder reads fall) and the named confound (the deduction moving to
`data_modeling` or `queries_and_live`).

#### 20.24.3 Calibration #15 on 0.2.31: the removal candidate's prediction confirmed; the capability lever's null by its own rule

**2026-09-20 — recorded** (`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-20-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.31/`; the
predictions, `issues/closed/20260919-pre-registered-predictions-for-calibration-15.md`,
filed before any lane produced a design and amended only before the run). The pin
carries W14a, W14b, W15b, W15c, Repair B and the oversized-sync recovery; not MC1.
Holistic **91** — the series' high, not a criterion: setup_and_docs 93, data_modeling
85, authorization 97, queries_and_live 89, writes 95, offline_sync 95, media 98,
background_work 90, client_integration 90, diagnostics 94, scale_and_limits 86,
operations 87. The grader (the other family, fresh) 100 pass / 1 fail / 18 not-run
of 119 — the not-run are M7 and the native-simulator artifacts #14's grader labelled
fails — one major defect, the application's typing indicator, none Snapback's; the
builder's 1 h 17 min paid no Snapback-attributed correction (twenty agent errors,
two environment, two ambiguous; a scale-time digest re-cut under `E_BOUND`, the
documented envelope; one `E_STORE database locked` racing a CLI job against the
host's time-index adoption — the second builder in a row to meet that class).

Scored against the file, category by category: **`setup_and_docs` 93 ≥ 91 —
confirmed**, 5.1 above the fitted line that had been falling at −0.37/run, with the
mechanism the design named (the README a cold builder reads 1,999 → 300 lines, the
design's −1,699; the guide output saved as evidence 2,905 → 3,451 across 8 → 14
cards; README + guide together −24%; the docs budget enforced at the publish gate)
and without the confound (`data_modeling` inside its null band, `queries_and_live`
above its threshold). **`queries_and_live` 89 ≥ 84 by the number, null by the rule**:
the grader did not probe the bound (no probe in the report or trace), and the file
says the category is then read as null with the mechanism carried by the lane's
four-host regression — red on main `6002c855aa`, green on the pin. **`data_modeling`
85 — void** (MC1 absent; at W15c's share, 85 < 86, null), inside its null band, its
scorer's deduction worded as three runs ago. The thirteen-run fit: the holistic's
drift −0.59 against sd 1.75; `setup_and_docs` −2.44 against 2.76 — **no longer beyond
one sd** (twelve runs: −4.08 against 2.77, the one category whose decline had
steepened at #14); `data_modeling` −4.55 against 2.36 and `queries_and_live` −5.27
against 4.46 still beyond it. Ruling 40's test is passed on its first application:
a removal, named and predicted before it ran, moved the category it predicted.

This record commissions nothing. For the author's next decision, in order of
evidence: MC1 (the `data_modeling` lever, pre-registered and waiting for its pin);
the CLI-versus-host store contention two builders paid; and a holdout probe of the
search bound, absent from two graders, for the next packet.

## 21. Derived data: six properties, and three holes that were not holes (2026-09-17)

The 0.2.27 calibration (holistic 89; record
`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-17-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.27/`) left an assessment the author
put as *excellent at the job, not yet elegant*: a rolling-window ranking cannot be
maintained; the ranking tables builders write instead are writable by any principal; a
digest over expiring rows needs a staging clone; live reads cost a serving architecture;
presence drops a push. The question put to a session was how to fix these **through
foundational properties from which the good outcomes follow**, rather than a recipe each.

This section records what that session ran and what it proposes. **Nothing here is
built.** The author ruled §21.6's six questions on 2026-09-17 (rulings 28–33, §14), taking
the recommended option on each; two of them commission a design or a review, not code. Live reads are already owned
by wave 13 (briefed, not launched when this was written; it drafts §20.22 and rulings
23–27 from `~/projects/snapback-next-20260914/BOUNDS-FINDING.md`) and are P1 below only
for completeness.

### 21.1 What ran on the published binary

As §20.20 and the bounds finding did, the forms were run rather than read about: eight
projects against the **published `snapback4-darwin-arm64@0.2.27`**, recorded with
schemas, scripts and raw output in
[`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-17-snapback4-derived-probes/`](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-17-snapback4-derived-probes/README.md).

1. **A rolling-24h ranking is maintained today.** Likes carry the post's hour and time,
   derived from the authoritative post inside the mutation; `maintain trend: count likes by
   postHour, postAt, postId` with `by byScore: postHour, value, id`; the query merges the
   last 25 hour lanes and filters the exact trailing edge (`where .postAt > now -
   86400000`). At 3,963 and at 31,937 posts in the trailing day it examines **265 and
   276** rows, is complete with `next`, answers in 2–3 ms over loopback, and its top ten
   equals a JavaScript oracle with no out-of-window row. It is fresh in the like's own
   transaction, its target is refused to every principal at `check`, and its cost is lanes
   × heads — independent of the window's population. Zero-like posts are a second bounded
   read (`posts[at > since] last 10 by byTime where trend[hour(.at), .at, .id] = 0`; 25
   examined). With `sync public` on the sources the query `runs: device`, every lane
   `held, no horizon`, and the like mutation still predicts; `N_MAINTAIN_SYNCED` names the
   cost. The form to publish copies one fact and checks it: the like stores `postAt`
   alone, the maintain groups by the computed `hour(.postAt)`, and `insert <- .principal =
   viewer and exists posts[id = .postId, at = .postAt]` refuses a false time with `E_RULE`
   (run on a small fixture; the scale numbers above are from the variant that also stores
   the hour).
2. **A digest needs no staging table.** A job's nested range seek over a table with
   `expire at` is admitted with no note. One helper —
   `count(messages[conversationId, at >= periodStart, at < periodEnd] first 100 by
   byConversationTime where not .tombstone and .senderId != reader and .ordinal >
   readOrdinal)` — serves the UI's inbox as the viewer and the job as the system; the job
   walks each profile's own memberships, so a chat the principal is not in cannot
   contribute; the chunk's commit is the exactly-once emission. Tombstoned,
   expired-but-unswept, own and out-of-period messages are excluded. **Seek the period and
   filter the ordinal, never the reverse:** for a hub in 1,000 chats of 99 messages with
   nothing read, the ordinal-seeking form parks at `E_BOUND` (16,384 examined; its work is
   the unread history) and the period-seeking form finishes in one chunk (its work is
   memberships plus the period's messages). The 0.2.27 builder's
   `E_STRUCTURAL_FANOUT` came from a cleanup *mutation* over their expiring staging table
   — the refusal blocked the detour, never the digest.
3. **A rule can pin a hand-kept score to the engine's count**: `insert <- exists
   likeCounts[postId = .postId, value = .score] or (.score = 0 and not exists
   likeCounts[.postId])`, and the same over `next` for `update`. A forged 999 is refused
   `E_RULE`; the true value passes. `update <- allow` was never required; the feeds card's
   own fresh-ranking recipe shipped it, and three builders copied it. (Wave 12, in review
   when this was written, withdraws that recipe.)
4. **Two surface refusals force the long forms**: a merge lane source may not be
   `range(a, b)` ("a merge for lane requires a bounded range read"), so the 25 lanes are
   25 lines; and a table index refuses a computed time key (`hour(at)`), which only
   maintain groups take.

So the mechanism exists for all three. What is missing is that **the first form a builder
writes does not land on it**, and that the pavement taught the detours: the README's
digest recipe is one sentence, the jobs card's one worked job walks a whole table by id,
the maintains card says a calendar day is not a rolling window and stops there.

### 21.2 The diagnosis

One cause sits under all five items: **derived data is second-class.** `maintain` — a
count or sum over row-local columns, kept in its own table — is the only derivation the
engine owns. Every other derived fact (a rank, an unread count, a feed, a digest, a push
decision) is materialised by hand into an ordinary principal-written table. That costs
design (tables, jobs, sweeps), safety (`update <- allow`, on the ranking table and, in
the 0.2.27 lane, on `posts` itself, because the builder keeps `posts.score` on the row),
freshness (a schedule's lag) and bounds (staging tables, then expiry refusals on the
staging tables). It is also precisely what a scorer reads back as workaround.

The remedy is not a new subsystem. General incremental view maintenance is out: a
derivation the engine keeps in the writing transaction must have an O(1) per-write delta,
and a fan-out belongs to a read (P1). What follows is six small properties, each one
sentence a builder could repeat, each deleting tables rather than adding a recipe. None
raises a ceiling or introduces a planner.

### 21.3 The six properties

**P1. Every read is total.** Wave 13's subject (rulings 23–27): an admitted read completes
or carries a continuation the SDK follows by itself; the author never writes a number the
schema states; costs are measured facts, not speculative notes. It makes "search every
chat" one merge with one cursor and search cost independent of messages per chat. For
Following it does less than this section first said: the bounds bar's builders measured a
merge-on-read Following at 2.5–3.1× on metered requests and 7.3–9.0× on bare clients at
500 over 5 follows (about +2.5 ms per 500-lane page), so the feeds recipe stays the paved
Following until wave 13 has measured keys-only heads. Nothing below substitutes for P1.

**P2. A fact about a row lives on that row.**

```quarry
table posts:
  authorId: principal
  at: time
  likeCount: count likes by postId        -- proposed: engine-kept, zero from birth, unwritable by any program
```

*If the group is a row, the aggregate is a column; if the group is a tuple, it is a
`maintain`.* Every caveat §20.20 attached to maintain-as-ranking — no row at zero, ties by
maintained id, a target that can neither expire nor be capped, a nested `posts[.postId]`
read in every shape — comes from the aggregate living in a separate table keyed by group
id. On the row: the post exists at zero; an ordinary index orders `likeCount, at, id`; the
count dies with the row, rides the row's audience and horizon, and arrives with `*`; and a
device that holds the post predicts the new count exactly, because holding the row *is*
knowing the base (the "unknown aggregate base" class disappears for this form). It is
what builders reach for first, and it removes the only reason `posts` was `update <-
allow`. The engine delta is small: `apply_aggregate`
(`crates/snapback4-core/src/engine/maintains.rs`) gets and puts one target row under
`Origin::Maintained`; the column form writes one field of the referenced row through the
same path — indexes, the partition log, and a cascade into maintains sourced on that
table (`profiles.likesReceived: sum posts of likeCount by authorId`), refused when cyclic.
Direct writes reuse the maintain-target refusal. First scope: the group is a reference to
this table's id; the source does not expire; adoption is the existing derived rebuild. The
cost to watch is one row event per contributing commit to the row's audience (the log
coalesces within a commit only); a latest-image-per-row coalescing of a stream page is
the follow-up if a measurement shows a hot row. This extends §20.20; it is not `system
update`: the derivation is declared, the engine owns the write, and no statement borrows
authority. The commissioned design is
[LLP 3000.007.001](./3000.007.001-maintained-columns.rfc.md) (ruling 30), and two blind
rounds have **corrected this paragraph**: the first build has no cascade; the total is not
kept only on the row but in a private, authoritative maintain that the column mirrors
(one answer for absent and expired parents, birth, overflow and redefinition); a device
does not derive the column until a sync-protocol defect the review surfaced is fixed; and
the "small delta" was not small — the reservation, the touched-table set, write results,
adoption and activation each needed a rule of their own.

**P3. Time is a key, never a predicate: a window is a contiguous range of buckets.** The
design already says this for rules (`expire at`; NOT-DOING, "Time in rules") and for
maintain groups (`day`, `hour`, `bucket`). Finish it: (a) a table index may lead with a
computed time key over an immutable time column; (b) a merge lane source may be `range(a,
b)` or a list, in queries, under the meter — the sibling of ruling 23's boundless prefix
lane source. With P2:

```quarry
  by byHourLikes: hour(at), likeCount, at, id                 -- proposed (a)

query trending(c: cursor ?):
  return merge posts after c last 10 by byHourLikes where .at > now - 86400000:
    [h] for h in range(hour(now) - 24, hour(now) + 1)         -- proposed (b)
```

Zero-like posts are included, ties break by post time, and there is no job, table, rule
or expiry. Bucketing is what makes this sound where a value index over an expiring source
is not (§20.9.3): **dead data is a contiguous key range the lanes never enter**, so
nothing stale is ordered, charged or swept under a reader. Per-entry expiring index
entries should not be built. An index word (`window 1d on at`) that lowers to exactly
this is optional sugar, to be decided on a measured run. The same property is the
digest's window (§21.1 item 2). One limit stands, and wave 12's investigation states it
carefully: a ranking by likes *arriving* in the last day on posts of any age has no
*stored* order that stays true, because its value moves without a write. That is unbuilt
rather than unsound for a `count`: expiry only lowers a count, so the stored value never
understates the live one and a threshold scan down the stale index terminates exactly
(wave 12's round-1 correction to §20.21). No such bound holds for a signed `sum`.

**P4. A program never needs to know that a table expires.** `expire` leaks into admission
(`E_STRUCTURAL_FANOUT`: "a mutation page cannot scan a table with expiry"), maintains
(`E_QUARRY_MAINTAIN_EXPIRE`, `E_QUARRY_LIVE_MAINTAIN_PROBE`), meters and residency. The
mutation-page refusal is §17's: expired rows are charged before the verdict under
`Cut::Abort`. For a **uniformly decided** prefix the verdict no longer depends on
candidates (§17's own repair), and the unswept rows are rows this viewer could read while
they lived; the residual bit is how many rows of the viewer's own group expired recently.
Proposal: admit a mutation page over an expiring table when its prefix is uniformly
decided, after a blind review: §4's principle 7 reserves any relaxation that touches a
disclosure channel for one. Non-uniform stays refused.
P3 removes most of the need for `expire` in windows, which shrinks the rest. **Outcome
(§21.9): reviewed twice and declined; the refusal stands, and P4 holds for queries and jobs.**

**P5. A table has one writer class, and the compiler knows which.** Principals through
rules; the engine through a declaration; the system through a job (rules `deny`). The
footgun is a column that is *derived in fact* — every program write to it is computed
from reads, none from an argument — under a rule that admits everyone. `check` can see
that by provenance rather than by name; wave 12's `N_PUBLIC_AGGREGATE_COPY` is its first
instance. Generalise the trigger to that definition, with two rewrites that both exist:
P2's column, or §21.1 item 3's rule. After P2 it should rarely fire.

**P6. Presence may defer an effect; it never drops one.** Grader finding d-05 of the
0.2.27 record: the server still held a closing long poll, so `not exists connected[p]`
was false and nothing was recorded. §19.1's grace machinery is the right one, stopped one
step short: when presence decides only effects, a *live poll* should be a short grace,
not proof. The effect is durable at commit, deferred a few seconds, and satisfied only by
an authenticated poll **arriving after the commit**. Code-traced
(`crates/snapback4/src/serve_changes.rs`, `serve.rs`): every commit wakes every held poll
(the wait is on the store's sequence), a live client "re-polls within milliseconds of an
answer, a dropped one never does", and each poll's arrival already records
`presence_reconnected`; a vanished client is noticed only when a 2-second heartbeat write
fails, which is the window d-05 fell in. "A currently live poll at claim" stops
satisfying. No poll and no grace stays immediate. Send-time against dispatch-time,
and the 35-second constant as application semantics, go away. The cost ruling 32 asked
for is measured (`presence-cost/` in the record, published 0.2.27, a 50-member room): 49
durable effect rows cost about 0.9 ms a send (about 18 µs a row); with 49 held polls a
send's p50 rises from 2.9 ms to 15–19 ms **whether or not it emits**, because every commit
wakes every poll; and under rapid sends today's grace path already recorded a deferred
effect for about 86% of connected recipients, who were between a poll's answer and its
re-poll. One durable effect row per recipient is affordable; no per-commit aggregate row
is needed (§21.8, L4).

### 21.4 What each item becomes

| Item | Today | After |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rolling ranking | an expiring rank table with a job or a source-write, 15–60 lines | one column, one index, a three-line query (P2 + P3); available today as §21.1 item 1 with hand-kept keys and typed lanes |
| Principal-writable derived table | `update <- allow` on the rank table, and on `posts` | the table does not exist (P2); a surviving copy is flagged with two working rewrites (P5) |
| Digest over expiring rows | 2 tables, 2 maintains, 4 sweeps, a cleanup mutation | about a dozen lines, available today (§21.1 item 2); P4 removes the refusal that met the detour |
| Live reads | the 253-line feeds recipe; the two-cursor search walk | P1: search is one merge, one cursor; Following keeps the recipe until keys-only heads are measured (wave 13) |
| Presence | a send-time guard loses a push inside the dead-poll window | P6: deferred, never dropped |
| A DM broken by leaving it (d-01) | a pair table and membership rows that diverge | not foundational: the chat template's `dm` gets or creates by pair key and re-joins; `remove` refuses a DM |

### 21.5 Sequence

**Wave 14 — no rulings; mostly the cards.** Two lanes were briefed
(`~/projects/snapback-next-20260914/W14a.md`, `W14b.md`, with `W14-LAUNCH.md`). W14a makes
the lanes ranking Trending's first answer, with its zero-like fill, its residency and its
limits, keeps the read-time sort for `rollingLikes()` only, and points
`E_QUARRY_MAINTAIN_EXPIRE`'s rewrite and the maintains card's calendar-day paragraph at
hour lanes. **The wave 12 lead verified the finding on its own build and ran W14a
unmodified inside wave 12 (lane W12R1c), before 0.2.28**: that wave's blind reviews had
independently found that the read-time-sort `trending()` fabricated its ranking past the
examined cut (an unfinished maintained point read coerced to 0), which the lanes form
dissolves rather than documents. W14a is therefore wave 12's, and §20.21 carries it.
W14b puts the period-seeking digest, with its hub envelope, on the jobs card and changes
that card's worked job to a range-seek sweep; it is briefed and not launched. Wave 13 is
the rest of the no-rulings work.

**After rulings.** P3(b), then P3(a), then P2 (the largest; core-owner work), then
P5, P6, and P4 after its blind review.

**Instruments, in parallel** — items 4 to 6 of the gap list are evidence, not design.
Native O4 sits at 10 because the run prompt waives simulator proofs and a codex sandbox
cannot reach CoreSimulator: run the published `witness ios` against the builder's app
from the lead's shell in the grade phase. M7 was skipped, not failed: the packet has the
phase. The builder's process-device snapshot — 341.9 MB of Node RSS and a 5.79 s cold
acquisition on the scale user — has one obvious first suspect, the single `public`
partition every device holds whole (48,200 posts at the Clique envelope): wave 13's
ruling 27 takes per-table cold rows and bytes first, then decides public subset sync. Flat search is wave 13's lazy matching lanes; digest work
proportional to the period is §21.1 item 2; the hub 2× gate should be run as a gate; the
p95 ceilings want one calibration against the September 5 cell, journaled, and the
midpoint rule a tolerance band.

**Measure** with wave 13's bounds bar (N ≥ 3; Trending and the digest are already two of
its four tasks), adding four counters: tables, jobs, rules that admit everyone on a
derived column, and numeric literals the author chose.

### 21.6 The rulings (2026-09-17)

Put to the author one at a time, each with its alternatives and a before/after; the
author took the recommended option on all six. §14 carries the binding lines.

| # | Question | Alternatives put | Ruled |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 28 | May a merge lane source be `range(a, b)` or a bounded list, in queries, bounded by the meter? | range only with a constant width; keep typed lanes | **Yes, both.** No static lane count: `check --cost` prints "lanes ≤ meter", N_WIDE_MERGE does not fire |
| 29 | May a table index lead with `day`, `hour` or `bucket` of a required time column? | computed columns (`postedHour: int = hour(.at)`); store the hour by hand | **Yes, as index keys only.** No new column, no new card word |
| 30 | Maintained columns — `col: count\|sum src [where ..] by <a reference to this table>` | fix maintain tables instead (`over posts`, carried columns); measure first | **Yes in direction; design and two-family blind review first**, built only on READY |
| 31 | Generalise `N_PUBLIC_AGGREGATE_COPY` to "derived in fact, admitted to everyone" | keep wave 12's trigger; make it an error | **Yes, after a false-positive census**; a note, never an error |
| 32 | Presence defers and never drops | card only (always emit; the device suppresses); accept the window | **Yes; measure effect rows on the chat fixture first** |
| 33 | Mutation pages over an expiring table when the prefix is uniformly decided | review purge-on-access instead; decline | **Commission the blind review**; land only on READY from both families |

Evidence that arrived with ruling 28: in the bounds bar's first "before" run a fresh
builder, with no card to copy, wrote hour lanes over a hand-made `hours` *table* to get a
lane source the language would accept — and met a compiler defect doing it
(`issues/20260917-snapback4-merge-lane-source-seek-expression-is-an-unknown-variable-at-runtime.md`).
Also measured while the rulings were put: a bounded list argument (`[a] for a in authors`,
`authors: [principal] <=50`) is refused as a lane source with the same message as `range`.

### 21.7 What stays out

No join views and no general incremental view maintenance. No `system update` (§20.20
stands). No expiring index entries. No third Trending recipe and no digest recipe with
tables. No ceiling raised. And the standing one, now paid for three times in four days
(§20.20, the bounds finding, this section): **no card, recipe or LLP section says "the
engine cannot" until someone has run it on the published binary.**

can overturn it; none publishes a package, raises a ceiling or lands an engine change
without its blind review.

Hedging stays: with `if not online: emit A` and `if online: emit B`, an unconfirmed recipient gets A deferred (cancelled if they return) **and** B. A fix session applies this with the review's other findings — an exponential, unmetered setup walk on alias chains; the server-only projection compiled into the wasm device; "never dropped" restated as durable admission — and the fixed commit goes back to both families | GPT showed L16's closing claim — that the change makes the server's charges independent of the recipient's connection state — is true only of the paved form: a payload that keeps its ordinary value makes a held recipient run work a graced one skips. The alternative that would make it true everywhere is for the **server to charge every safe presence arm as prediction does**, so that presence never changes a meter on any host. That changes what absent and graced mutations are charged today, reaches every effects-only mutation and the cards' cost statements, and is therefore **put to the author rather than built here** |
| L18 | After W15a's second blind review (Grok READY with no findings; GPT NOT READY on two points): **the invariant this lane owns is "in every meter field, a held recipient costs the server at most the larger of what device prediction reserves and what a graced recipient costs"** — the projection never gives the server a way to exceed prediction that a graced recipient did not already have. It replaces L17's "at most what prediction reserves". A second fix session applies it as one assertion over every fixture, adds an ignored boundary reproduction of the rule-probe divergence, and makes setup discovery linear (round 1 removed the exponential; GPT showed Ω(N²) remains). The fixed commit goes to both families a third time | "Server at most prediction" is false **on the base engine** for reasons that have nothing to do with presence: a device trusts its synced reads and the server charges their rule probes (3 against 1 on the 50-member send), besides the six heap bytes of a two-principal guard. Both are one ticketed defect class, identical for held and graced recipients. GPT called it pre-existing and said to admit it explicitly or repair metering; repairing it would change what absent and graced mutations are charged, which this lane may not do |
| L19 | The wave 12 lead's ticket — a maintain value index cannot break ties by a group column — is taken into this program with a **recommendation: one rule, "a prefix of group fields, then `value`, then any remaining group fields, then `id`"**, as lane W15c after W15a and W15b, with a two-family blind review; *not launched*, because it changes the language surface and the author may prefer to wait for maintained columns, which need no maintain table for a ranking at all (§21.11) | Calibration #12's builder adopted the hour lanes and still kept a hand-written score, because a maintained ranking cannot be newest-first on ties. Group fields are fixed for a maintain row's life, so trailing ones cost key bytes and no extra index rewrite. Ordering ids by time (the ticket's option 2) would give meaning to an order the cards call opaque |
| L20 | After W15a's third blind review (**both** families NOT READY on the same single High): L18's inequality is not closed under composition — a two-recipient absence guard, which carries the base engine's ticketed six heap bytes over prediction, followed by a positive-presence emit, which prediction charges and a graced recipient never runs, puts held above both by exactly those six bytes. The invariant is restated as what is true: **the projection reserves nothing new — every arm a held recipient newly executes is a safe presence arm prediction already charges — so a held execution can exceed prediction only by the base engine's own ticketed under-reservations on the statements it executes.** A third fix session pins that program as a both-store witness written to stay green when the ticket is fixed, removes a last Θ(D·N) case from setup discovery and a whitespace error; no charge changes on any host | Three rounds have each broken a numeric inequality by composing the projection with a defect the base already had; both reviewers' smallest fix is to say so rather than to repair metering inside this lane, and the lead agrees: repairing the six bytes changes what prediction or every absent and graced mutation is charged, which belongs to the ticket, under its own review. The witness makes the ticket's fix visible the day it lands |
| L21 | After W15a's fourth blind review (Grok READY with no findings; GPT: no High, the invariant and its witness sound, nothing disturbed, one Medium): **setup discovery's bound is O(S log S) in the program's size, accepted.** The walk is server-only, unmetered because a device does not run it, and runs once per execution over a program whose size the compiler bounds; exponential (round 1), quadratic (round 2) and Θ(D·N) (round 3) are removed; a balanced-tree regression pins the shape (1.3 ms at 1,024 leaves, 120 ms at 65,536, on a loaded host). A fifth, confirmation-only round reads the one commit that records this | The lane's briefs said "linear" where they meant "not superlinear in a way a program's author can trigger". GPT named accepting the bound as the smallest resolution; the alternative — amortised-linear scoped indexing — is more code in the presence proof machinery for no observable gain |
| L22 | After W15a's fifth blind review (Grok READY, nothing at any severity; GPT: no High, behaviour and records preserved, one Medium and one Low about the lead's wording of the bound and of a test's purpose): both corrections are applied — the bound counts the site-string bytes its maps compare, which the metered planner has already charged — and **a sixth round is the last. Recorded before it runs: a finding about what the code does stops the landing; a remaining finding about how the lead words a bound or a test's purpose is applied as the reviewer writes it, recorded, and the lane lands** | Rounds 1–3 found real defects (an exponential walk, a quadratic one, a Θ(D·N) one, server-only code in the device, three numeric invariants false on the base engine) and were worth every minute. Rounds 4 and 5 found no behaviour to change; each NOT READY named "accept this bound explicitly" as its smallest resolution. A bar that a careful reviewer can hold open indefinitely on the lead's prose is not the bar the author set; the rule is fixed before the round so that it is not the lead grading its own lane |
| L23 | **Lane W15a lands.** Round 6: GPT READY with no findings at any severity, Grok READY with nothing above Low and no finding about behaviour — READY from both families, which is the bar; L22's fallback was not needed. Landed as one commit over main with its twelve review records (`llp/reviews/3000.007-w15a-presence-defers{,-2,-3,-4,-5,-6}.{gpt,grok}.md`); **nothing is published by this session** — the wave 12 and 13 leads, who own the next publish, are told | Six blind rounds. What they changed: an exponential, then quadratic, then Θ(D·N) unmetered walk under the engine lock became O((S + P) log S) with P metered; server-only machinery left the wasm device (470,404 → 467,632 B gzip); three numeric invariants the lead wrote were each false on the base engine and became one statement about the change (*the projection reserves nothing new*) with a both-store witness; "never dropped" became durable admission. What they did not change: the behaviour ruling 32 asked for, which was right from the first implementation — held equals graced in every measured field for the paved form, prediction untouched, and through the commit's own wake all 49 connected recipients of a 50-member send end `satisfied` with zero worker attempts |
| L24 | **Ruling 31's lane (W15b) is briefed and held, not launched**, although its one precondition (0.2.28 published) is met. It goes after the value-index tie-break (L19) or maintained columns, whichever the author chooses; this session launches no further lane | The generalised note's first rewrite is "keep the count in a maintain and read it". Calibration #12 showed a builder who did exactly that and still kept a hand-written score, because a maintained ranking cannot break ties newest-first (§21.11). A note whose advice the builder cannot follow is noise, and the census would be measuring false positives against a rewrite that is about to change. Everything else in the queue — the tie-break's language change, accepting LLP 3000.007.001, building it after wave 13's horizon repair — is the author's to start |
| L25 | **Gate 2 of LLP 3000.007.001 passes on main after wave 13's horizon repair (0 rows held beyond the horizon, against 1,165 on published 0.2.28), so L14's ordering is satisfied once that repair ships.** Nothing else moves: the design's Status stays `Review`, acceptance is the author's, gate 5 is the build lane's milestone zero, and the oversized-page ticket remains a prerequisite of the first build | The re-measurement uses the workload pinned before the first measurement, through the real device rather than the wire, so that "the device no longer keeps them" is observed and not inferred from a landing message |
| L26 | **Ruling 31's "derived in fact" is data dependence, not information flow.** A column is derived in fact when every value written to it is computed from reads (and ambient values) — an argument may *select* which read-derived value is written (a branch arm, a `± 1`, a sorted or sliced pick, how many rows a loop sums) without disqualifying it; only an argument that flows into the value itself (`score: amount`, `score + amount`, a field copied from an argument) does. Lane W15b's brief said the opposite ("any program argument flows in"), and two review rounds, consistent with that brief, silenced exactly the shape the ruling exists for: the like/unlike toggle on a hand-kept score (calibration 12's `posts.score`, calibration 10's `trendingRows.score`, the four calibrations' copies of our own recipe's `feedPullCounts.used`). The lane's next session implements this reading; the reviews judge it stated | The footgun P5 names is a count *kept by hand* under a rule that admits everyone: the caller cannot set it to 999, but can nudge it by any declared mutation, and the repair — a maintain, or the pinning rule — applies exactly. Under the information-flow reading the note fires only on the rarest form (a copied scalar) and is silent on the toughest (a toggle), which is the 0.2.26–0.2.28 builders' form three times over. Precision was bought at the cost of the purpose; a definition that names the purpose is the one to keep |
| L27 | **W15b's review has a stopping rule, recorded before its fourth round runs** (as W15a's had, L22): after the round-3 fixes — one Medium behavioural finding (a caller-supplied upsert id classified as minted) and message wording — a fourth round reads the fixed head; a finding about what the code does stops the landing; a remaining wording finding is applied as the reviewer writes it and the lane lands | Three rounds found real defects (a panic, an exponential join, an unbounded shape, three optimistic selections, one wrong rule — the lead's) and each was worth its round. The remaining surface is a diagnostic's prose. The bar is fixed before the round so it is not the lead grading its own lane |
| L28 | **L26 amended: cardinality is provenance of its own.** W15b's fourth round — both families, independently — showed L26's clause "how many rows a loop sums" was too broad: `count(xs { … })` over an argument list stores `count(xs)`, and `for i in range(0, amount): delta = delta + 1` stores `amount`; in both the caller supplies the number, and can set 999 in one call. A collection carries a cardinality provenance beside its items' data: READ for a read page, ARGUMENT for an argument list or an argument-bounded range, AMBIENT for a literal; shaping, filtering, slicing and sorting preserve it; `count`/`len` return it as data; a loop accumulation over a domain takes the domain's. A finite choice among ambient constants stays selection. Under L27 the round-4 finding stops the landing; the fix is one more session and a fifth round, confirmatory | Two families reaching the same rule from two constructions is the strongest evidence a review loop produces; the rule is now stated in the terms the pass computes (data, control, cardinality), which is what makes the next round able to confirm rather than construct |
| L29 | **Loops prefer silence.** W15b's fifth round (one family READY; the other one Medium: a conditional reset inside an argument-bounded loop let the join drop the domain's cardinality) is the fifth loop or join shape the pass had modelled optimistically. The loop rule is made shape-independent: every binding assigned anywhere inside a loop body takes the domain's cardinality into its data — ARGUMENT for an argument list or argument-bounded range, UNKNOWN for an unknown domain, READ for a read domain — regardless of resets, joins, breaks or position. The accepted cost: an unconditional reset inside an argument-bounded loop (which stores 1) is silent — a false negative on a program no builder writes. **The sixth round is the last**: a wording finding is applied and the lane lands; a behavioural finding is met by extending the same conservatism (the construct goes UNKNOWN) and the lane lands without a further round | A note's only unacceptable outcome is a note on a caller-supplied value; precision inside loops bought nothing but review rounds. The bound on rounds is stated before the round, as L22 and L27 were |

### 21.9 Ruling 33: the proposal, and its blind review

Ruling 33 commissioned a blind review of one relaxation: a mutation page over a table with
`expire at`. **Round 1 (2026-09-17, baseline `0e3cf8959`): GPT NOT READY, Grok READY WITH
CHANGES** ([gpt](./reviews/3000.007-ruling-33-expiring-mutation-pages.gpt.md),
[grok](./reviews/3000.007-ruling-33-expiring-mutation-pages.grok.md)). The proposal as first
put — admit any uniformly decided page, predicting or not, on the argument that a query
over the same prefix already shows the same viewer its unswept population — did not
survive:

| Finding | Family | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **High.** The argument fails for authorization established *inside* the mutation: a viewer who may write a hidden gate row enables it, binds the expiring page of that gate's room, disables it; abort-versus-commit then shows the unswept population of a prefix no query by that viewer could examine | GPT 1 | Accepted, conservatively: a new condition, *a stable verdict* — no write to a table the page's read rule depends on (each table its `exists` probes name, and a probed maintain's source) can execute before the page's scan: none earlier in statement order, none inside a loop that encloses the scan. The broader contract — transaction-local authorization as an accepted disclosure — is **not** proposed; it would be the author's ruling |
| **High.** A predicting mutation can fail locally with `E_BOUND` on a device-only unswept row and never reach a server that would have committed it (`admit` converts only `Prediction` errors into a queued write) | GPT 2, Grok 1 | Accepted: **never-predicting mutations only**. Such a mutation is not executed on a device at all |
| **Medium.** "The mutation aborts iff the query is cut" is false at scan entry: an aborting page pays one attempt unit a query does not | GPT 3, Grok 2 | Accepted: the equivalence is restated for a scan-only mutation, after the attempt unit, other ceilings non-binding, the query asserted `cut == 'meter'` |
| Missing fixtures: the same addressed prefix under a denied verdict; the gate program; traversal through bound values, helpers, predicates, condition setup, nesting, shapes and serialized IR; sweep then a fresh retry with the failed receipt unchanged | GPT 3 | Accepted |
| **Low.** `RuntimeError::Bound`'s `Display` still names `actual`; `refusal()` and the receipt do not | Grok 3 | Accepted as a landing note |
| Purge-on-access and a free skip are unnecessary for soundness | both | Agreed; neither is proposed |

Both Highs were raised against a proposal the lead believed sound, and the first by one
family only. **Revision 2** — (a) uniformly decided, (b) never predicting, (c) a stable
verdict, (d) a plain index, no `matching`, no merge — went back to both families the same
day.

**Round 2: GPT NOT READY, Grok READY WITH CHANGES**
([gpt](./reviews/3000.007-ruling-33-expiring-mutation-pages-2.gpt.md),
[grok](./reviews/3000.007-ruling-33-expiring-mutation-pages-2.grok.md)).

| Finding | Family | What it shows |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **High.** A stable verdict is not a stable examined population. With `read <- .owner = viewer`, a blind update the viewer's write authority admits can set a hidden row's `owner` to the viewer and its expiry to the past; a page over the viewer's own prefix `[viewer, 'A']` then examines that row or does not, and `E_BOUND` versus the program's own refusal reveals the hidden row's retained column. D(page) is empty — the rule has no probe — and a snapshot query of the prefix is empty in both worlds | GPT 1 | The page's *table*, and every maintain source that can change it, must also be unwritten before the scan |
| **High.** Core schema validation admits a maintain sourced from a maintain, which Quarry refuses; a one-level D(page) then misses the engine's write to the inner target | GPT 2 | The dependency set is a fixed-point closure, or core validation refuses what Quarry refuses |
| **High.** "Statement order" and `Flow` do not see every write the interpreter can run before a scan: `rows = { update gates[..]; typing[room] first 50 }` is a write inside an expression; a scan's filter block repeats per candidate with no enclosing `for` | Grok 1, GPT 3 | (c) has to be the interpreter's evaluation order over every `Stmt` and `Expr` form, repeated regions included, refusing any form it does not match |
| The fixtures place pages, not writes-before-scan; equality alone passes under blanket refusal; "no predicted rows" does not prove non-execution | both | — |
| "Never executed on a device at all" overstates: a device query entry can select a mutation by name | GPT 5 | — |

**Decision (the lead's, under the author's delegation; §21.8 L10): the relaxation is
declined and the refusal stands.** Ruling 33 asked for a review and set the bar at READY
from both families; two rounds did not reach it, and each round found a *new* way for a
mutation to shape, inside its own transaction, what its page then measures — first the
verdict, then the population. What would now have to be built is an exhaustive
evaluation-order effect analysis with repeated regions, a closure over maintain sources,
and protection of the page's own table, to admit one narrow class: a never-predicting
mutation that pages an expiring table before it writes anything relevant. The one observed
need (§21.1 item 2) is met without it — a job may read a table with expiry, and the digest
needs no staging table — and lane W14b makes the refusal's rewrite name that door.

What the reviews established stays useful. A query's cut and a mutation's abort are not the
same observation once hidden-target write authority (§8.7) exists, because only a mutation
can change the state it then measures; any future relaxation starts from GPT's smallest
fix (no effective write to the page's table or to its verdict's closure may execute before
the scan, in interpreter order) and from never-predicting only. §21.3's P4 is therefore
narrowed: *a program never needs to know that a table expires* holds for queries and jobs,
and P3 removes `expire` from windows; a mutation page over an expiring table stays
refused, deliberately.

**Narrower replacement accepted (2026-09-27).** LLP 3000.025 replaces the
blanket expiry refusal with the sufficient condition of a never-predicting
mutation's initial wholly read-only statement prefix; the fixed-point
dependency and precise evaluation-order analysis stays declined, the prior
attacks and historical decision remain recorded, and P4 no longer means a
mutation page is expiry-blind.

### 21.10 What the maintained-column gates found (2026-09-17)

Ruling 30's design read READY from both families (§21.8 L13), and its §7 asks for three
measurements before a lane is briefed. Two were taken by proxy on published 0.2.27 — the
hand-kept count column puts exactly the image the design's mirror would — and are recorded
in [`column-gates/` and `horizon-gap/`](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-17-snapback4-derived-probes/README.md)
and LLP 3000.007.001 §7.1.

- **The form has a price, and it scales with the parent.** Per like, to every holder of the
  row: 823 bytes on the wire for a 500-byte post against 329 for a synced maintain row and
  182 for the like alone; 4,323 against 329 for a 4,000-byte post. Over 100,000 likes the
  store grows 567 MB, 115 MB and 98 MB. P2 stands — a fact about a row lives on the row —
  with its cost stated in bytes rather than in adjectives.
- **The horizon gate fails: 58.3% against 10%.** On the pinned workload a device that
  acquired `last 2000 by byTime` is sent 1,165 posts beyond its horizon in a third of a
  day, because the stream applies no horizon and a device stores every `put`.
- **And that growth is a wrong answer today.** The device's coverage rule assumes its rows
  on the horizon index are a contiguous newest suffix. After two likes on old posts the
  published client returned `p09 p08 p04` where the server returns `p09 p08 p07`, then
  `p03 p02 p01`: three posts skipped, every page `complete: true, fresh: true`. Filed P1
  (`issues/20260917-snapback4-device-page-past-horizon-answers-from-scattered-rows.md`).
  It is a P1-class violation in §21.3's sense — a read that lies about completeness — and
  it is older than this program: the hand-kept `score` and any edit to an old row cause it.

The repair the ticket sketches is P3 applied to residency: **a device knows a horizoned
group exactly at and after the boundary key it acquired, and nothing below it.** The
boundary is a key, recorded once at acquisition, never a count to be maintained; coverage
by key replaces the `has_more` heuristic; `apply` declines an unheld row below the key.
Nothing evicts and nothing back-fills, so NOT-DOING's "exact horizon maintenance" stays
refused.

Decision L14 (§21.8) sets the order: the horizon repair first, maintained columns after it.

**Verified after the repair (2026-09-17, main `c2823d4954`).** Wave 13's lane W13g landed
it the same day (§20.22.7). The lead re-ran its own probes against a build of that commit
rather than take the landing on report: both reproductions now return the server's pages
(`p09 p08 p07`, `m09 m08 m07`), and the pinned gate-2 workload, run through the real wasm
device and counted in the device's own row store, leaves **0** rows beyond the horizon
where published 0.2.28 leaves **1,165**. Gate 2 passes. The server still sends those
images; the device no longer keeps them or answers from them.

### 21.11 What calibration #12 showed (0.2.28, 2026-09-17)

From the wave 12 lead's record of the first calibration to carry this program's two recipes
(`benchmarks/runs/2026-09-17-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.28/`):

- **The digest card worked.** `background_work` rose from 86 to 91, the best of the series,
  and the friction review files "digest staging detour avoided — documentation plausibly
  prevented recurrence": the 0.2.27 builder hit `E_STRUCTURAL_FANOUT`, built a non-expiring
  staging table and blamed itself; the 0.2.28 builder did not repeat it (§21.1 item 2, lane
  W14b).
- **The hour lanes were reached and only half adopted.** The builder consulted the feeds
  card and implemented the 25 lanes, then **kept a hand-written `posts.score`** beside them.
  The reason is a limit, not a preference: the brief wants equal scores newest first, and a
  maintain value index accepts group fields, then `value`, then `id` and nothing else, so a
  maintained ranking's ties are by maintained id, always
  (`issues/20260917-maintain-value-index-cannot-tie-by-a-group-column.md`). A derivation can
  be fresh, indexed, bounded and unwritable by principals and still fail an ordinary product
  requirement.
- The series as a whole is **declining**, not flat, once fitted over all ten Astra-medium
  runs (the wave 12 lead's correction, `5fd848de55`): `scale_and_limits` and
  `queries_and_live` lose the most. Nothing in §21 relied on "flat".

This is the strongest consumer evidence yet for P2. A count that is a **column of the row**
is indexed like any other column — `by byHourLikes: hour(.at), likeCount, at, id` with
ruling 29's computed key — so the tie order is the author's to write, the copied `postAt`
and the rule that validates it disappear, and the lanes are read from `posts` itself. Until
then decision L19 (§21.8) recommends the small language fix.

## 22. Common application shapes (LLP 3000.007.002)

The Draft commissioned by Charlie Cheever on 2026-09-17 — "write up an LLP for
this, or a sub-LLP if more appropriate" — is
[LLP 3000.007.002](./3000.007.002-common-application-shapes.rfc.md): the
completion criterion for search, counts, rankings and periodic work, the
sequence, and the builder experiment that would measure it.

It was first written as a section of this document, carrying a hand-rolled
"Section status: Draft" line to say that this RFC's Accepted status did not
extend to it. §21.8 L2 had already decided that question earlier the same day,
for ruling 30's design, and recorded its reason: *a design that must pass Draft →
Review → Accepted needs a status of its own, which a section of an Accepted RFC
cannot carry.* That is why LLP 3000.007.000 and LLP 3000.007.001 are numbers
rather than sections, and it is why this is one too. Nothing in the proposal
changed in the move.

## Non-claims

- Every clock, loop count and holistic score above is N = 1 calibration
  from the named record, read for shape. Nothing here is a magnitude claim
  about a backend, and no measurement establishes a matched backend
  comparison. Probes, reference witnesses and builder calibrations are
  distinct kinds of evidence with distinct sample counts; each retains its
  named package and workload, and none of them measures lifecycle behaviour
  that was not run.
- This bullet held, as written, only while the document was an unruled
  proposal. It no longer describes it: §14 carries thirty-three rulings,
  several of which commission designs, blind reviews and lanes, and §17–§21
  record what those lanes landed and published. What still holds is the
  narrower promise the rulings were made under — **no relaxation raises a
  ceiling, and none widens a disclosure boundary without a two-family blind
  review that reads READY** (§4, principles 2 and 7; the record of it is
  §15, §17, §19.4, §21.9 and the wave reviews of §20). Ruling 33 is the
  worked example of that promise being kept against the lead's own
  proposal: reviewed twice, not READY, declined.
- It does not reopen the language decision (LLP 2000.000 §4.1); it
  finishes the flip that decision presupposed.
